## Page 95

Software Engineering (IT632)
Core Course (3-0-2-4)
MScIT Sem-II
Prerequisites
Students should have familiarity with data structures and basic algorithms, and exposure to at least
one mainstream programming language.
Course Description
This course introduces the fundamental principles, concepts, and practices of Software Engineering as an
engineering discipline. It emphasizes systematic approaches to software development, maintenance, and
evolution, preparing postgraduate students to design, develop, and manage complex software systems
in professional and research environments.
Course Objectives
• To develop a principled understanding of software engineering as a systematic, disciplined, and
quantifiable engineering practice, enabling students to distinguish between ad hoc programming
and professionally engineered software systems.
• To equip students with the ability to analyze, model, and specify software requirements rigorously,
accounting for technical constraints, stakeholder needs, and socio-technical factors inherent in
real-world systems.
• To cultivate competence in applying architectural and design principles to construct high-quality
software systems, with explicit consideration of trade-offs among performance, scalability, main-
tainability, security, and cost.
• To instill an engineering mindset for ensuring software quality through testing, measurement,
reviews, and controlled change, emphasizing risk management and long-term system evolution
rather than short-term functionality.
• To foster ethical, professional, and managerial responsibility in software engineering practice,
preparing students to make informed decisions that recognize the societal, legal, and economic
impacts of software systems.
Key Topics
• Introduction to Software Engineering
• Software Processes and Lifecycle Models
• Iterative and Agile Development
• Requirements Engineering Fundamentals
• Requirements Elicitation and Specification
• System Modeling and Software Architecture
1

## Page 96

• Software Design Principles
• Software Construction Practices
• Software Testing Fundamentals
• Software Quality and Metrics
• Configuration and Change Management
• Software Project Management
• Emerging Trends in SE
Grading Components
• Class test, In-sem & End-sem exam, lab assignments, group project.
Evaluation Scheme
• Lab(50%)
– Assignments & Labs: 20%
– Team Project: 30%
• Theory(50%)
– Class Tests: 10%
– In-Sem Exam: 20%
– End-Sem Exam: 20%
Course Outcomes
• Analyze complex software problems and justify the need for systematic software engineering ap-
proaches by distinguishing between programming-centric solutions and engineering-driven pro-
cesses applicable to large-scale, long-lived systems.
• Elicit, analyze, and formally specify software requirements by applying appropriate requirements
engineering techniques while managing ambiguity, stakeholder conflicts, and evolving constraints.
• Design software architectures and detailed designs that balance competing quality attributes,
demonstrating informed trade-off decisions related to performance, scalability, maintainability,
security, and cost.
• Apply software quality assurance techniques—including testing, reviews, metrics, and configura-
tion management—to evaluate and improve software artifacts, emphasizing defect prevention and
controlled system evolution.
• Estimate, plan, and manage software projects using foundational project management principles,
including effort estimation, scheduling, risk assessment, and team coordination.
• Critically evaluate software engineering practices, processes, and tools in real-world and emerging
contexts, including agile development, DevOps, and AI-assisted software engineering, recognizing
their limitations as well as benefits.

IT694 Computer Networks (Winter’2026)
Instructor: P.S. Kalyan Sasidhar, 2109, Faculty Block-2
Course Placement: MSc IT, Semester-II
Course format: 3 Lecture hours and 2 Lab hours.

Course Content:
This course will cover the fundamental principles of wired computer networks
with focus on layered architecture, protocols, implementation and issues specific
to the Internet. The objective is to provide an understanding of how the Internet
works which include how data flows from a source to a destination. Focus will
also be laid on certain topics such as network design and automation. network
management, administration and debugging which are ideally required for
networking IT professionals.

The associated laboratory component is designed to expose students to the basic
LAN hardware and configuration, and network simulation tools for the analysis
of traffic and network protocols. Cisco laboratory exercises will be strongly
referred to provide an industry level exposure to real life networking scenarios
based labs.

Textbook:
1. James F. Kurose and Keith W. Ross. 2016. Computer Networking: A Top-
Down Approach (7th ed.). Pearson.

References:
1. Douglas E. Comer. 2013. Internetworking with TCP/IP (6th ed.). Addison-
Wesley Professional.
2. Andrew S. Tanenbaum and David J. Wetherall. 2010. Computer Networks
(5th ed.) Prentice Hall Press, Upper Saddle River, NJ, USA.
3. Larry L. Peterson and Bruce S. Davie. 2011. Computer Networks, Fifth
Edition: A Systems Approach (5th ed.). Morgan Kaufmann Publishers Inc., San
Francisco, CA, USA

## Page 98

Evaluation Plan:

Course outcomes:
At the end of the course, students are expected to:
1. Understand the TCP/IP Internet reference Model
2. Learn commonly used network protocols and their design
3. Be able to design and develop network applications
4. Be exposed to traffic engineering and multimedia networking concepts
5. Measure performance of protocols using analytical methods and
simulation tools

IT628: Systems Programming, Winter 2025-26
Course handout

Instructor: Anish Mathuria
Lectures: CEP 207, Monday 10-10:50, Wednesday 10-10:50, Friday 8-8:50
Practicals:  TBA
Prerequisites: programming in C

Course abstract

All operating systems provide services for application programs they run. The service
points through which programs request services are called system calls. Both system
calls and library functions exist to provide services to application programs. A library
function may invoke one or more system calls and in many cases perform the same
action as a system call. However, it is important to realize that the library functions may
be replaced if desired, whereas the system calls usually cannot be replaced. This course
provides an introduction to the system calls for performing essentials tasks like
managing processes, files, memory and network communication.

Topics to be covered

Process management: programs and processes, creating processes, waiting
for processes, executing new programs, zombies, Unix shells.

Signals: Sending signals, signal handlers, signal blocking, signal sets, job-
control signals, non-local jumps.

I/O management: file abstraction, file descriptors, file access, file permissions,
reading directories standard I/O: buffering, implementation using low-level
I/O, I/O redirection.

Inter-process communication: pipes, non-blocking reads and writes, shared
memory.

Threads: differences between processes and threads, creating threads,
terminating threads, reaping terminated threads, detaching threads.

Concurrency: race conditions, locking, semaphores, synchronization with
semaphores, producer-consumer problem.

Sockets: types of connection, addressing, creation of transport end point,
sending and receiving messages.

Learning outcomes
At the end of the course, students will

Be familiar with the design and implementation of Unix shells

Be familiar with writing programs that create, manage and terminate processes
and threads on UNIX.

Be familiar with the relationship between standard I/O and low-level I/O.

Be familiar with the problems that may arise because of concurrent execution of
multiple processes or threads.

Be familiar with using sockets to implement interprocess communication.

## Page 103

IT637 Introduction to Algorithms (Winter 2026)

Instructor: Amit Mankodi, 4111, Faculty Block-4

Course Placement: MSc. IT, Semester-II

Course format: 3 Lecture hours and 2 Lab hours.

Course content: This course intends to provide a rigorous introduction to fundamental
techniques in the design and analysis of algorithms. The course aims to cover the asymptotic
notation, divide and conquer techniques, solving recurrences, greedy algorithms, dynamic
programming, sorting and searching algorithms, depth-first search, breadth-first search,
topological sort, minimum spanning trees, shortest path problem, pattern matching, NP-
completeness, and approximation algorithms.

Text Book:
Introduction to Algorithms, 2nd edition, by Cormen, Leiserson, Rivest, Stein (CLRS)

Evaluation plan:
1. Insem1 exam: 15%
2. Insem2 exam: 20%
3. Endsem exam: 30
4. Lab Evaluation: 20%
5. Pop-up quizzes: 10%
6. Attendance: 5%

Course Outcomes:
After successful completion of the course:
•
Students will learn various approaches, such as recursion, dynamic programming,
backtracking, etc, to designing an algorithm.
•
Students will learn searching and sorting algorithms.
•
Students will learn advanced data structures such as trees and graphs and their
respective algorithms.
•
Students will be able to perform comparative analysis of algorithms using the time and
space complexity.
•
Students will be able to apply logical thinking to solve problems by applying designed
algorithms

## Page 106

Course Title
Interactive Data Visualization
Course Code
DS612
Credit Structure
3-0-2-4 (L-T-P-C)
Category
Core
Semester
Winter Semester (AY 25-26)
Program
M.Sc(Data Science)
Prerequisites
Basic understanding of data and statistics
Course
Objectives/
Brief Course
Description
The world is increasingly relying on data collection/analysis for the decision
making process.  Presently, an enormous amount of data gets generated every
day. However, human beings have relatively constant perceptual and cognitive
abilities. It is always difficult or almost impossible to notice mistakes within
a dataset and that makes data visualization particularly important. Data
visualization provides a powerful way to communicate data-driven findings
and motivate interesting analyses. Effective visual encoding can supplement
cognitive calculations with simple perceptual inferences and improve
comprehension and decision making process. Good visual representations
help engaging more diverse audiences in the process of analytic thinking.

In this course, students will learn the foundations of information visualization
and design. Lab assignments using Tableau will sharpen their skills in
communicating using data. Goal of the course is to explore the important
concepts in human perception, decision-making, color theory, and storytelling
as the students get hands-on-experience while applying the concepts in lab
assignments / course-project.
Short assignments will help students get comfortable with the various
techniques we discuss during lectures. The assignments will be done using a
real-dataset. Project will give an opportunity to students to apply visualization
to a dataset of their choosing.

Evaluation/
Grading Policy
Exam: One Mid-Sem and end-sem examination. (20 + 35 marks)
Lab Assignments: There will be around 6-7 Lab assignments in the whole
course comprising a total 30 marks. Lab attendance is compulsory. Lab
attendance will carry 20% marks in each assignment.
1 course Project: 15 marks (Project will be evaluated in multiple ways
including project presentations of all students).
Grading scheme is relative and depends on both: class performance and
minimum expectation from a student.
Course
Materials/
References
1. Visualization Analysis and Design by Tamara Munzner
2. Data Visualization by Kieran Healy (https://socviz.co/)
3. Edward R. Tufte , The Visual Display of Quantitative Information
4. Tableau (www.tableau.com/learn/training/20222)
5. B. Shneiderman. The eyes have it: a task by data type taxonomy for
information visualizations.

APPENDIX: Detailed Course Content (Module-wise)

Module 1
Introduction to Data Visualization and Getting Started with Tableau, what
and why, perception and cognition,
Module 2
Data Abstraction - Data types, Dataset Types (Tables, fields etc), Attribute
Types (Categorical, Ordered etc), Semantics
Module 3
Task abstraction - Actions and Targets, Analyzing and Deriving
Module 4
Marks and Channels - Using Marks and Channels, Visual Channels and
encoding, and Channel Effectiveness.
Module 5
Arrangements by Keys and Values, Express Quantitative Values,
Separate, Order, Align, Spatial Axis Orientation, Spatial Layout Density.
Module 6
Arrange Spatial fields - Geometry, Geographic Data, Scalar Fields, Vector
Fields.
Module 7
Colors - Theory, HSL, colormaps, categorical vs ordered color, color
palettes, color deficiency.
Module 8
Manipulate View: Change View over Time, Select Elements, Navigate-
facet into Multiple Views. Reduce Items and Attributes, hierarchical and
multivariate data.
Module 9
Data visualization choices, design principles, presentation and storytelling
techniques using multiple visualization idioms such as Scatterplot, bar and
line chart, streamgraph, stacked bar chart, Gantt charts, slope graphs,
heatmap, histogram, box-plot, radar plot, pie and coxcomb chart, Sankey
diagram, ridgeline plot, radar plot, glyph maps, SPLOM, parallel

## Page 108

coordinates, choropleth, symbol maps, cartograms, dot density maps,
isosurfaces, tooltips, small multiples, MAUP and more.
Case Studies
Anscombe’s quartet, John Snow's work on the cholera outbreaks, Charles
Minard's chart, visualizing distributions, Bias in Visualization - Misleading
visuals, data bias, Uncertainty Visualization, animated bubble charts such
as Gapminder Tools and many more for data analysis and storytelling.
Based on availability of time and how the courses progresses, data-visualization based
thematic paper discussion are also included in the lecture-sessions.

DS614 Big Data Engineering (Winter ’2026)
Instructor: Ankush Chander, 3209, Faculty Block-3
Credit Structure (L-T-P-Cr): 3-0-2-4
Course Placement: M.Sc Data Science, Semester-III
Course content:
This course aims to equip students with a deep understanding of the architectural and theoretical foundations of big data systems, moving from storage internals
(LSM-Trees, B-Trees) to modern Data Lakehouse architectures. It establishes a rigorous framework for distributed computing, examining replication, partitioning,
and consistency models (CAP, PACELC) required for fault tolerance. Students will develop engineering proficiency in designing orchestrated batch and streaming
pipelines while mastering probabilistic algorithms (HyperLogLog, Count-Min Sketch) for large-scale approximation. Finally, the course emphasizes reliability
engineering, inculcating best practices for data quality, schema governance, and observability in production environments.
Course uses lab and project work to ensure the balance between theory and practice, ideas and their application.
Suggested Textbook/references:
Evaluation plan:
1. 2 examinations(mid sem and end sem): 40%
2. Lab submissions and Project work: 60%

Analyze complex distributed storage requirements to select optimal data architectures (Lakehouse vs. Warehouse) based on trade-offs between consistency,
availability, and latency.
Design scalable data ingestion and orchestration systems that integrate batch and streaming data (Kafka/Airflow) to meet specified throughput and latency
constraints.
Apply mathematical principles of distributed computing and probabilistic algorithms (HyperLogLog, Count-Min Sketch) to solve large-scale cardinality and
frequency estimation problems.
Evaluate the impact of partitioning, replication, and concurrency control strategies on system fault tolerance and linearizability in distributed environments.
Develop automated data governance and reliability mechanisms (schema contracts, observability) that address professional responsibilities regarding data
quality and integrity.

## Page 115

Course Abstract: PC 753 Fundamentals of Filmmaking for MDes CD

1
COURSE FILE

PC753: Fundamentals of Filmmaking

Credit Structure: 2-0-4-4
Program: MDes (Communication Design)
Year: 1
Semester: 2
Duration: 16 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio)
Timing: 9 AM – 6 PM

Course Instructor: Anirban Dutta Gupta

Couse Placement: Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 2. This is a specialised course in
design and is aimed at integrating inputs provided in previous courses in semester 1 and 2
into a ‘multimedia’ matrix and orient the students to its multimodal application.

Course Format: Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio
sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio
sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap.
Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time
demonstrations and guidance by faculty members. Students will be expected to conduct
fieldwork.

Course Objective and Content:
The terms film and video is used interchangeably.

The objective of this course is to introduce the various aspects of filmmaking – from concept
till production. The course will synthesize and integrate the theoretical inputs the students

## Page 116

Course Abstract: PC 753 Fundamentals of Filmmaking for MDes CD

2
have received in course ‘Multi-modal storytelling’, technical and creative inputs during
‘Basics of Photography’ and ‘Image, Text, Sound’ course and expose them to the professional
methodology and structured approach of constructing a filmic narrative and its execution,
leading to a final finished film – whether it is a fictionalized story or a story from the real
world in the documentary genre.

This course not just aims to introduce the students to the complex world of film production
but to also introduce them to complicated and multi-modal production workflow where
different mediums have to be seamlessly integrated to create a complete product. While
film and filmmaking, as a medium, is extraordinarily valuable; the core learning from this
course is to gain greater appreciation of planning, budgeting, logistics, human-resource
management, storytelling and narration and integrating different visual (and auditory)
mediums into a coherent communication.

Course Abstract:
The art of filmmaking is a complex process requiring perfect synthesis of multiple elements
from domains of storytelling, narrative, photography, design, technology, physics and
management. This course will give an overview and introduction to this world and expose
the students to the various elements that go into conceiving, planning and executing a
motion film project.

One of the aspect that the course will look at in detail is ‘continuity of filmic time and space’
and hopefully realize that it is a grammar that has come about, based on our natural sense
of editing, and which has been adapted to the art and craft of filmic space. To understand
this better the course will look at the history of filmic narrative construction, tracing from
actuality films of Lumiere Brothers through Edwin S Porter and D W Griffith, Pudovkin’
theories of Constructive Editing and Eisenstein’ Thematic Editing and Intellectual Montages.
One will see how these early masters explored and laid down the basic grammar of film
narrative which is followed even today, though with modifications to assimilate the current

## Page 117

Course Abstract: PC 753 Fundamentals of Filmmaking for MDes CD

3
refined film sense. The course will look at the counterpoints placed by Andre Bazin and his
appreciation of the neo-realist film form. One will also see how, historically, the Indian film
industry (not just Bollywood) has matched and mirrored the developments in Europe and
the United States.

Based on this historical overview the course will explore the various principles of filmic
construction – the narrative, storytelling, continuity, smoothness, cutting point, image size
and angles, sense of direction, overlapping, cross cutting.  The course will explore the idea of
‘real’ time versus ‘reel’ time and the illusion of ‘continuousness of narrative’ or continuity.
We will also look at temporal and spatial continuity and how these can be maintained while
translating it into a video/film production. We will see how cinematography, lighting, sound
and editing all assist in the creation of continuity.

The course will try and assimilate these inputs and explore how they can be incorporated
into a video production. To achieve this, the students will script, storyboard and direct short
fiction films and documentaries. The execution of these films will be done in a professional
manner following the three-stage film production routine: pre-production stage, production
stage and post production stage. In the process, the students will experience the intricacies
of film production, production management, art direction, cinematography, acting etc.

An overview will also be provided on a typical studio set-up. The course will also be
exploring video as a technological medium and compare the pros and cons of video
production versus the film production with special emphasis on how to work around the
restrictions posed by video, and how using various optical tricks can ‘cheat’ the minds-eye
into believing that the quality of product is better than what it is. In this regard, the course
will explore cinematography using the video camera and basic lighting techniques.

Students are encouraged to recall the learnings from the Photography module as there are
multiple overlaps between photography and filmmaking.

## Page 125

Course PC 731 Ethnography and Applications 3-0-0-3

Course Instructor - Vishvajit Pandya

Course Objectives :Contemporary Communication Design informed by multi-media
technologies are  meant to be immersive, engaging and meaningful experiences. The
method of Ethnography which derives from the discipline of Anthropology  teaches students
to understand cultures, communication and social activities at large  through, immersive
exposure and participant-observation. This makes it  particularly relevant to the discipline of
Communication Design.  Ethnographic analysis provides powerful tools to understand how
communication practices, tools and systems inform and impact social relations and cultural
practices.

Course Content :

The module introduces the foundations and basic methods of ethnography derived from the
fields of visual anthropology and social and cultural anthropology. It will look at several
applications of these disciplines to current communication practices. Students would be
expected to undertake fieldwork assignments and related applied activities such as
collection of empirical data, qualitative data, contextual inquiry and usability testing that
provides the foundations for user-experience design and visual design. The objective of the
module is to provide the student the intellectual tools to develop research capacity for the
final design projects they would have to undertake.

Students will be also required to read and be prepared for in-class presentations and
discussions of their readings and how these speak to their field experiences.

Evaluation

## Page 126

There will be continuous evaluation for this module, and include, in-class readings and
presentations, field work and the writing and presentation of field reports and research
papers.

Course outcome

At the end of the module, students will be able to acquire a deeper understanding of user-
research beyond the usual field survey or AI driven analytics. They will deploy their
ethnographic research insights to develop better user-profiles and journeys and therefore
design more ethically, effectively and responsibly.

## Page 127

Course
Code
Course Name
Instructor: Dr. Jenson Joseph
PC724
Multi-Modal Storytelling
Winter 2025-26
Course Outline:
This module will introduce students to the crucial role of storytelling in
communication practices. The module will explore how narratives are interwoven
into various media, encompassing verbal and written language, gestures, music,
visual art, interactive media,   and time-based audio-visual format. In the
contemporary digital landscape, narratives have evolved into new and complex
forms and applications. A multimodal narrative is a form of storytelling that
seamlessly integrates multiple modes of communication, such as text, images,
audio, and video, to create a richer and more immersive experience as well as
sensitizing the students to the requirements of different formats of storytelling.
This approach harnesses the strengths of various media to enhance emotional
and cognitive engagement, capturing the audience's attention and leaving a
lasting impression. By integrating diverse formats, we can express ideas in a
multitude of ways, catering to varied storytelling styles and making information
both memorable and accessible. Contemporary digital media space offers
enormous opportunities for many innovative narrative formats such as the
"micro-narrative” or serialized storytelling where the user is allowed to engage
with the content in bite-sized pieces. All this requires a deft and agile
understanding of user needs and the craft of multi-modal story-telling. By the end
of this module, students will cultivate a deep understanding of both the thematic
and modal aspects of storytelling. In other words, they will explore the structure
of narratives and the art of their telling across different media, within diverse
social and cultural contexts.

Course Abstract: PC 754 Introduction to Photography for MDes CD

1
COURSE FILE

PC754: Introduction to Photography

Credit Structure: 1-0-4-3
Program: MDes (Communication Design)
Year: 1
Semester: 2
Duration: 13 Days (Full Day – Lecture + Studio)
Timing: 9 AM – 6 PM

Course Instructor: Anirban Dutta Gupta

Couse Placement: Core course for MDes (CD) Semester 2. This is a specialised course in
design and is aimed at integrating inputs provided in previous courses in semester 1 and 2
into a ‘multimedia’ matrix and orient the students to its multimodal application.

Course Format: Module based. The course is taught as a combination of Lectures and Studio
sessions. Lectures introduce concepts, which are then analysed and explored via studio
sessions and assignments. Lecture and studio is integrated and in certain cases can overlap.
Teaching is through hand-on learning and through learning-by-doing and via real-time
demonstrations and guidance by faculty members. Students will be expected to conduct
fieldwork.

Course Objective and Content:
The Photography course will aim to provide a holistic understanding of photography as a
subject of historical study, a medium of capturing the world and as a communication tool. A
variety of photographic approaches, both current and historical, will be explored to allow
you to understand the medium in its multiple representative forms.

## Page 129

Course Abstract: PC 754 Introduction to Photography for MDes CD

2
The course will introduce ‘why’ and the ‘how’ of photography – with ‘why’ covering the
creative and communication aspect of photography, while the ‘how’ will define the technical
aspect of achieving it.

The technical and creative development of photography through time will broadly cover the
mechanics of generating an image and the function of the camera using the DSLR as the
representative device. The focus will be on the digital medium of photography against the
more traditional chemical medium, and its subsequent image manipulation using software’s
like Photoshop.

Course Abstract:
Photography, in simple terms, is the method of recording images by the action of light or
similar related radiation on a material or device specially designed to be sensitive to it.
The term ‘Photography’ comes from the word Photos=Light and graphein=to draw and was
coined by John F W Herschel in 1839. By that count, the world of photography is little more
than 180 years old. In the past century and half -  technology, science and art of
photography has expanded dramatically. However, the concept of photography is even older
than this. In 1000 AD Ibn Al-Haytham invented the pinhole camera - the Camera Obscura.
Interestingly the law of optics was observed even earlier by Aristotle in 330 BC. Therefore,
one can safely say that it is more than 2 millennia of quest that has led us into today’s world
of photography.

The first ‘photograph’ was taken in 1827 by Joseph Niepce. This is a defining moment as
before this the Camera Obscura was for observation and drawing, for example, by the
renaissance painters who used it for accurate reference of perspective. Niepce was the first
one to actually record the event on an engraving plate coated in bitumen. This was the
beginning of experimentation on chemical photosensitive agents eventually leading to the
photographic camera and film.

## Page 130

Course Abstract: PC 754 Introduction to Photography for MDes CD

3
From its beginning as a method of observation till today - photography is seen as a medium
of expression as well as a science of documentation. Fields of photography extends from
photography as an art form, photography as a product, photography as documentation,
photography as evidence, photography as science and photography as experiment.
This module on ‘Introduction to Photography’ will explore certain key aspects of
photography and build on previously learnt skillsets to relook framing an image using the
camera - through observation, content, context, composition, colour, and light -  primarily to
understand and build an aesthetic sensibility. The students will learn to train the eye for a
perfect composition through the viewfinder and learn to effectively capture expressive
moments with accuracy and aesthetics to develop a sense of storytelling through still
images.

In parallel, this module will also introduce the mechanics of taking a photograph. The digital
camera, its various settings and its interplay, usage of light and image correction and
manipulation, the role of the exposure triangle. The objective would be to not just provide
aesthetic input but to also provide the technical inputs – concept of aperture, ISO, shutter
speed, depth of field – so that what is imagined and seen can be effectively captured.
The students will also be exposed to Adobe Photoshop for post-production processing of
images.

At the end of the course the students will put up an exhibition of their selected work for the
community to see.

Students will be given various assignments through which they will explore the concepts
discussed in class.

Readings will be given to the students which they are expected to complete during the
course. The learnings from this course will be continued and built upon in the Filmmaking
module.

1
PRINCIPLES OF INTERACTION DESIGN
(Interactive Spaces and New Media)
M.Des Semester II, DA-IICT
Course Code : PC752

Course Overview:
Interaction Design can be understood as the design of interactions between users and their artefacts. While it
is often thought of in terms of software products, interaction design is a broad field that includes elements
such as motion, space, sound, image and more.

Thinking past the boundaries of mobile or desktop interactions, this course encourages students to look at the
physical world and the possibilities of creating new immersive experiences and environments.

Introducing technologies such as gesture and voice controls, hologram projections, 360 video, augmented,
virtual and mixed realities the course will challenge students to understand how designing for interactive
spaces diverges from small-scale interactions of mobile or web design, to encompass constrains of scale,
medium, environment, and whole body utilization.

The course has been structured as a mix of lectures, short exercises and a semester project that will enable
students to engage with design issues involving interaction through practical application.

In the semester project, students will undertake the creation of an interactive space. They will work
individually and at times in groups, to investigate this area in the context of interaction design.  Each student
will by the end of the semester be expected to frame his/her individual proposal and develop an innovative
design prototype.

DAY 1 : Feb 17th Monday
Lecture Session: Introduction and Approaches to Interaction Design
a. What is interaction design?
b. Everyday examples
c. History and interdependent areas of design
d. User Centric Design
e. Activity Centric Design
f. System Design
g. Genius Design

Discussion: Five Dimensions of Interaction design
a. Words
b. Visual Representations
c. Space
d. Time
e. Behaviour (Appearance & Texture, Sound)

Reading:
 Chapter 1: What is interaction design? in Saffer, D. (2010). Designing for interaction: Creating
innovative applications and devices (2nd ed.). Berkeley, CA: New Riders.

## Page 140

Masters of Design in Intelligent User Experience Design – Mdes (IUxD)

Semester -2

MI611:

Design Thinking: Concepts & concerns

Credit structure: 2-0-2-3

Content: This course explores Design Thinking as a structured, human-centered approach to
problem-solving, fostering creativity and strategic innovation. Students will engage with key
methodologies, including stakeholder mapping, creative matrix, schematic diagramming, and
problem tree analysis, to develop solutions that address real-world challenges. The course also
examines the creative economy and the role of design in business strategy. By applying design
thinking principles, students will produce system design and decision-making (DM) plans and
craft compelling product/service pitches using business canvas models and go-to-market
strategies. Through hands-on exercises and case studies, students will develop critical thinking,
collaboration, and problem-solving skills essential for applying design thinking in various
professional contexts. This course equips students with practical tools to drive innovation and
create meaningful user-centered solutions.

Course Structure:  Module submission: 60%
                                 End Term Jury: 40%

_____________________________________________________________________________

MI612:

Design Research Methods

Credit structure: 2-0-2-3

Content: This course provides a comprehensive understanding of design research, emphasizing
both quantitative and qualitative methodologies. Students will explore key UX research
techniques, including diary studies, desk research, ethnographic research, surveys, field studies,
card sorting, contextual inquiry, tree testing, and focus group interviews. The course equips

## Page 141

students with essential research tools, enabling them to systematically engage with users, experts,
and environments to gather valuable insights. By learning structured research approaches, students
will develop the ability to analyze data effectively, identify user needs, and inform design
decisions. Practical application of these methods will help them produce well-structured research
reports that support user-centered and evidence-based design solutions. By the end, students will
be proficient in conducting rigorous research, ensuring that design outcomes are both meaningful
and impactful.

Course Structure:   Module submission: 60%
                                  End Term Jury: 40%

______________________________________________________________________________

MI613

AI & Interaction Intelligence

Credit structure: 2-0-4-4

Content: This course explores the fundamentals of Artificial Intelligence (AI), focusing on its
concepts, capabilities, and concerns. Students will gain an understanding of how AI models
function, the types of data required, and their impact on design and interaction. A key focus is on
Generative AI (GenAI) and its applications in creative problem-solving and design innovation.
The course also introduces interaction intelligence, examining how AI enables conversations
between objects and humans. Through conceptual models and hands-on exploration, students will
learn to integrate AI into design workflows, enhancing user experiences and interaction design.
By the end, they will have a strong foundation in AI-driven design thinking, enabling them to
conceptualize and develop intelligent, user-centered solutions.

Course Structure:   Module submission: 60%
                                  End Term Jury: 40%

 _____________________________________________________________________________

## Page 142

MI614:

Intelligent Design Decisions

Credit structure: 2-0-4-4

Content: This course explores the principles of information design and data visualization,
focusing on transforming complex data into meaningful and user-centric representations. Students
will learn to analyse, categorize, and visualize data in context to stakeholders, ensuring clarity and
usability. The course covers data collection methods, representation techniques using tools like
Tableau, and cognitive walkthroughs to assess user interaction with information. Additionally,
students will explore infographics, signages, and wayfinding systems to enhance communication.
Through hands-on exercises, they will develop skills in structuring data for effective storytelling
and decision-making. By the end, students will understand the concept of data manifestation and
be able to create visually compelling, insightful, and functional information designs that enhance
user experiences.

Course Structure:   Module submission: 60%
                                  End Term Jury: 40%

______________________________________________________________________________

MI615:

Human Factors (Physical and Cognitive Ergonomics) in Interaction Design

Credit structure: 2-0-2-3

Content: This course explores the critical role of human factors in designing user-friendly digital
products and interfaces. Students will learn about physical and cognitive ergonomics, focusing on
how users interact with technology in terms of comfort, efficiency, utility, and safety. Key topics
include cognitive load management, anthropometry in digital products, and the impact of human
factors on display design, communication, and behavioural design. The course also examines how
human-centered design reduces errors and enhances user experiences. Through a redesign process
informed by ergonomics, students will apply their knowledge to create intuitive, accessible, and
efficient digital solutions. By the end, they will develop a deep understanding of how human
factors influence interaction design, enabling them to craft more effective and user-centric
interfaces.

## Page 143

Course Structure:   Module submission: 60%
                                  End Term Jury: 40%

MI616:

User Interface Design

Credit structure: 2-0-4-4

Content: This course provides a comprehensive understanding of User Interface (UI) design,
focusing on creating intuitive and intelligent digital interactions. Students will explore the AEIOU
framework for user analysis, core UI design principles, and platform-specific considerations for
mobile, web, and kiosks. The course covers essential concepts such as Information Architecture
(IA), user journey mapping, and wireframing across low, medium, and high fidelity. Additionally,
students will engage in A/B testing to optimize user experiences and refine digital interfaces.
Emphasis is placed on crafting seamless digital experiences through structured grids, layouts, and
style guides. By the end, students will be able to design, prototype, and evaluate user-friendly
interfaces, producing wireframes, IA structures, and interactive digital prototypes that align with
user needs and industry standards.

Course Structure:   Module submission: 60%
                                  End Term Jury: 40%

EL527 ASIC Design (3-0-2-4)

Course Placement: ASIC Design is an Elective course for SEMESTER II M.Tech, ICT in
VLSI and Embedded Systems program and SEMESTER VI/VIII B Tech (ICT and CS)

Course format: It is 3 hours lectures and 2 hours of laboratory every week.

Prerequisite courses:   Basics of VLSI

Course Overview:

VLSI (Very Large Scale Integration) technology has emerged as a very important technology in
modern electronics featuring deep sub-micron manufacturing processes, low voltage operations,
exploding speeds and smart programmable devices sufficient enough to digest ambient conditions to
extremes. The electronics industry worldwide is rapidly approaching another revolutionary leap in
the global market scenario.  Semiconductor technology has crossed the quarter-micron threshold,
making tens of millions of transistors available on a single chip equipped with the powerful arm of
VLSI design.  This imparts the electronics industry a potential to create designs of incredible densities
and lightning speeds while utilizing batteries to power them. This has had a phenomenal impact on
widespread applications ranging from consumer electronics, communications, and defense to just
about everything.

As part of Electronics India program by Govt. of India, most of the electronic products and
semiconductor ICs are planned to be made in India. This skill development program in VLSI Physical
design will help to generate skilled manpower in IC design and manufacturing.

Course Content:

In this course, the individuals shall learn how to implement a design from RTL-to-GDSII. It will start
by coding a design in VHDL or Verilog. Then simulate the coded design, followed by design
synthesis and optimization. Further, run equivalency checks at different stages of the flow. After
synthesizing the design, it will be followed by floorplan, and place-and-route the synthesized netlist
while meeting timing. Gate-level simulation shall be run throughout the flow. Finally, write out a
GDSII file.

Books and literature:

There is no text book for it. Students will use the material prepared by the instructor and online
references.

Assessment method: Mid-semester, End semester examination, research based project and
related presentations.

Course Outcomes:

Learn about the various ASIC architectures, ASIC design flow, issues in ASIC design and
verification and its fix. The following are the highlights:

1. Describe architecture based on application specifications. Identify circuit topology. [P1 – P5]
2. Derive, design and define circuit parameters. [P1 – P5]

## Page 152

Page 2 of 2

3. Write synthesizable RTL code. [P1-P3, P5]
4. Perform synthesis, process design flow, CTS, routing etc. [P1-P5, P9, P12]
5. Verifying and validating the integrated circuit at various stages of design. [P1-P5, P9, P12]
6. The final step is generating the GDSII file of the design that is sent to the foundry for fabrication.
[P5]

## Page 153

Course Title
VLSI Testing and Validation
Credit Structure
3-0-2-4
Course Code              ED322 / EL464
Program

B.Tech/M.Tech
Category

Core/Technical Elective
Prerequisite courses  Digital Design, Basics of VLSI

Course Objective
The course is designed to cover the fundamental aspects of VLSI testing
and provide a strong fundamental understanding of test basics: the causes and behavior of
manufacturing defects in VLSI technology; defect statistics, fault models; test generation for stuck-
at faults, scan design-for-test (DFT), built-in self-test (BIST), memory test, etc. The process of
design verification where the basic verification principle and verification methodologies will also
be covered. The basic idea is to equip students to envisage testing and verification in a larger design
space like SoC.

Suggested Textbooks
1. M.L. Bushnell and V.D. Agrawal, “Essentials of Electronic Testing”, Kluwer Academic
Publishers, 2000.
2. L-T. Wang, C-W. Wu and X. Wen, “VLSI Test Principles and Architectures”, Morgan
Kaufman Publishers, 2006.

Reference Books
1. H. Fujiwara, “Logic Testing and Design for testability” MIT Press, 1985.
2. N.K. Jha and S. Gupta, “Testing of Digital Systems”, Cambridge University Press, 2004.

Grading
In semester exams- 35%
Final exam – 40%
Project – 25%
Attendance – 70% compulsory to take end semester exam and for project grading
Program Outcomes
At the end of the course student will be able to
 Apply the concepts in testing which can help them design a better yield in IC design.

## Page 154

 Tackle the problems associated with testing of semiconductor circuits at earlier design
levels so as to significantly reduce the testing costs.
 Analyze the various test generation methods for static & dynamic CMOS circuits.
 Identify the design for testability methods for combinational & sequential CMOS circuits.
 Recognize the BIST techniques for improving testability.

Sensors and Instrumentation (EL495)
Course: Sensors and Instrumentation (EL495)
Credit Structure: (L-T-P-Cr): 3-0-2-4
Course Placement: Technical Elective
Prerequisite Courses: Fundamentals of Electronics and Circuits, and Basic Measurement Techniques.
Instructor: Ajay Beniwal (ajay_beniwal@dau.ac.in)
Couse Content:
Unit
Content
Lectures
Unit 1 Introduction to Sensors: Sensor measurements, Sensing measurand, Sensor transfer
characteristics, Sensor characteristics: Sensitivity, Linearity, Resolution, Hysteresis,
Repeatability, Accuracy, Principle working of various sensors technology, Sensor
design optimization methods.
10

8

14

Relevant research papers from scientific publisher like IEEE, IOP, ACS, AIP, Elsevier, RSC and others.

Course Objectives
• Understand the fundamentals of sensors, sensing measurands, and sensor characteristics.
• Learn sensor instrumentation and interface electronics for system-level design.
• Explore fabrication, advanced sensors and IoT-enabled applications.
Course Outcomes
• Identify and analyse appropriate sensors for different measurands and applications.
• Design and implement sensor systems with appropriate interfacing and measurement techniques.
• Apply knowledge of advanced sensors and intelligent sensing systems in real-world applications.

Evaluation: Mid-Sem Exam – 25%, Quiz/Assignment – 15%, End-Sem Exam – 30%, Laboratory – 30%.
Laboratory Experiment: The laboratory component includes experiments on sensor measurements, signal
conditioning, sensor fabrication, testing, and IoT-enabled applications to provide hands-on experience,
reinforce theoretical concepts, and develop practical skills in sensor interfacing, measurement, and intelligent
sensing systems.

Advanced Wireless Communication CT548 (3-0-2-4)

Instructor: Dr. Manish Kumar

Course Placement: It is a Technical elective course for MTech first year (second semester) students and
BTech third year (sixth semester) students.

Course Pre-requisite: Fundamental understanding of courses like Probability and Statistics, Signals and
Systems, and Digital Communication.

Course format: It would be 150 minutes’ lecture and 2-hour lab session (coding) every week.

Course content: The course primarily starts with the evolution of wireless technologies, followed by
wireless channel modeling and representation. Later, important concepts like MIMO, OFDM which emerged
as key technologies for 4G and 5G wireless communications are discussed. Important aspect of channel
estimation for wireless communication systems will be discussed in detail. Subsequently, the Massive
MIMO and milli-meter wave systems will be discussed for next-generation wireless communication
systems. The prime motive of the course is to provide an understanding and background in the domain of
wireless communication which could pave the way forward to academic research in various areas of wireless
communication. Certain modules of the course could be helpful and serve as an important pre-requisite in
the relevant companies working in the domain of wireless communication.

 Few Text Books and Reference Books:

1. “Wireless Communications”, Andrea Goldsmith, Cambridge University Press.

2. “Fundamentals of Wireless Communication”, David Tse & Pramod Vishwanath, Cambridge

3. “MIMO Wireless Communications”, Ezio Biglieri et. al., Cambridge University Press.

4. “Wireless Communication Principles and Practice” T Rappaport

Assessment Method: Continuous evaluation will be carried out throughout the semester with Mid-Semester
Examination, End-Semester Examination and some weightage to the assignment. There might be a very
small change in this assessment method but in that case it would be duly notified well in advance.

Laboratory Component: Students will be given weekly problems which need to be simulated in
MATLAB. Focus will be on the different components of the system under study. Tentatively, a report
containing the results need to be prepared for evaluation. The students can either use the institute MATLAB
or use a personal license. The idea is to enhance coding expertise and understanding of wireless and digital
communication system and their modeling.

 Course Objectives:

 After the successful completion of the course it would lay forward the foundation towards working
upon further academic research problems and assignment in the broad domain of wireless
communication and aligned research areas.

 The candidates will gain important theoretical concepts whose knowledge is mandatory for fresh
graduates working in the relevant wireless communication and associated industries. The MATLAB
programing learnt during the practical sessions would be of prime help in correlating and better
understanding of the fundamentals of wireless communications.

 Problem Analysis and Design of Systems: The candidate would be able to utilize the concepts learnt
in the course in various principles of wireless communications like algorithm design, performance

## Page 157

evaluation and coding assignments in Industry which requires theoretical understanding of the overall
wireless transceiver architecture.

 Course Outcomes:

 To enable the students to develop a strong background in wireless communication, understand the
overall wireless system model and use the tools learnt in order to solve the complex algorithmic
design problem.

 Apart from further academic research in wireless communication where the course would be a good
background to proceed further, the knowledge would of significant help if the graduates look forward
to work in the relevant wireless communication industries be it 5G system engineering team,
algorithm, Modem system design and several other relevant teams as well.

 The objective is to enables the graduates to take individual responsibility and work as a part of a team
towards the fulfillment of both individual and organizational goals in such assignments where the
subject knowledge can act as a major advantage.

Lecture Schedule:

2.5. SVD Based MIMO Transmission

3. OFDM Systems: [6 hours]
4.1 Multicarrier Modulation and Transmission
4.2 Introduction to OFDM

4.5 Performance Evaluation of OFDM Systems

7. Prerequisite
courses
Signals and Systems or Introductory Knowledge of Calculus
8. Foundation for
Communication and Signal Processing, Machine Learning, Applied Mathematics
Algorithms, EVD.
9. Abstract Content
After a few minutes in a restaurant we cease to notice the annoying sound of
surrounding conversations, but a sudden silence reminds us of the presence of
neighbors. Our attention is clearly attracted by transients and movements as
opposed to stationary stimuli, which we beautifully ignore in our auditory
perception. Concentrating on transients is probably a strategy for selecting
important information from the overwhelming amount of data recorded by our
senses.
This course will prepare students in wavelet signal processing algorithms such as
signal (image) compression, signal denoising, edge detection, etc.
Suggested
 Text book(s)
S. Mallat,  A Wavelet Tour of Signal Processing. 2nd or 3rd Edition, Academic
Press, 2009.

  P. P. Vaidyanathan, Multirate Systems and Filterbanks

## Page 160

2

 E. Kreyzig, Introductory Functional Analysis and Applications.

   DIMENSIONALITY REDUCTION METHODS, SPECIAL SECTION, IEEE Signal
Processing Magazine, Vol. 28, Issue 2, 2011.
 https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/xpl/tocresult.jsp?isnumber=5714377

Deep Learning in Wavelet-Domain - https://arxiv.org/pdf/1811.06115.pdf

Detailed Course Contents
Topic Name
Content (2 -3 lines per 4 – 6 lectures)

1. Fourier Analysis (Kingdom): Basic review of Signals and Systems and Related
Calculus: LTI Operator (System) and Impulse Response, Fourier transform in
L1(R) and its properties, Defining Fourier Transform in L2(R), via Density
Extension, Global regularity of signal and spectrum decay, 2-D Fourier transform
and its properties. Gibbs Oscillations.

2. Sampling and Reconstruction, Shannon’s three-step sampling paradigms and its
limitations, Removal of Aliasing, Generalized Sampling Theorem in Hilbert Space
Framework, Compressive sensing.

3. Wavelet vs. STFT Frames and Applications: Concept of frames (linearly
dependent vectors) and application to noise reduction, Design of Sigma-Delta
Analog to Digital Converters, Communications, etc.

4. DSP Hardware Architectures: Radix-2 FFT, and DWT.

5. Review of Basic Linear Algebra and Functional Analysis: Metric Space, Vector
Space, Linear Combination, Basis, Frames, Normed Space,  Banach Space, Inner
Product Space, Hilbert Space Introduction to Hilbert spaces, L2 norm, inner
product, L2(R).

6. Time-Frequency analysis and wavelet: Spectrogram vs. Scalograms, Wavelets:
CWT, Admissibility condition, real vs. analytic wavelets, Application of Wavelets to
Instantaneous Frequency (IF) Estimation

7. Wavelet Zoom:  Lipschitz Uniform vs. Pointwise Regularity, Vanishing Moments of
Wavelets, Detection of Singularities, Multiscale Edge Detection, Multifractals,

## Page 161

3

Fractal Dimension, Singularity Spectrum, Modeling of Hydrodynamic Turbulence.

8. Multirate DSP: Upsampling, Downsampling, Quadrature Mirror Filterbank (QMF),
Paraunitary Filterbanks, Wavelets and Filterbanks.

9. Feature Vector Dimensionality Reduction for Machine Learning- Binning, Log-
transform, Principal Components Analysis (PCA), Eigenvalue decomposition,
Discrete Cosine Transform (DCT), DWT.

10.  Wavelets in Machine Learning: Wav-KAN: Wavelet Kolmogorov-Arnold
Networks,  Wavelet Scattering , Designing Learnable Wavelet Filters, etc

Program
Outcomes and
Objectives
1) The course builds foundation for the signal processing algorithms, such as short-time
Fourier transform (STFT), Gabor transform and wavelet transforms (ie., CWT and
DWT).
2) In addition, it will also discuss recently developed auditory transform and it’s
applications in speech processing.
3) In course evaluation scheme, project work is also considered. Students will be asked
to choose any one of the project topic and develop algorithms to implement the
objectives of project. This will help students to apply and appreciate the theoretical
concepts discussed in the class to real practical problems of engineering and
technological interest.
4) One of the important outcomes of this course will be to equip skills to read and
understand research papers related to the area of wavelets and time-frequency
analysis. This will be quite useful to the students who would like to go for higher
studies in near future.
5) This course will be of great value for those students who would like to do their thesis or
project work (e.g., BTP, masters, doctoral) in various image and speech related
pattern recognition, machine intelligence and signal coding applications.

## Page 162

IE416 -Robot Programming

Course Placement: Robot Programming is an ICT and Technical Elective course for 3rd year students of BTech
ICT, RAS (Robotics and Automation Systems) minor programs. It is also offered as an elective for MTech
program.

Course format: It is 3 hours lecture and 2 hour lab every week.

Prerequisites: Python Programming, C++ Programming, and Embedded System Software

Course content: This course introduces students to the robot arm, autonomous car, and ROS (Robot Operating
System) as well as numerous available tools VSCode, Python, OpenCV, TensorFlow, and GitHub. Course focus on
theory and hands-on, whereas hands-on will focus on applications and implementations motor, camera, GPS,
computer vision, and artificial intelligence. Students learn how to create robot model, interface to sensors and
actuators, and integrate AI/ML algorithms for autonomous robot. The course works through exercises involving a
number of autonomous robots (i.e., ground) that students could eventually use in their future robotics research and
innovation. The course consists of two parts which are (i) interactive lecture section, (ii) lab exercises on ROS
which will provide a platform to understand a real robot from scratch.

Text Book: Yoon Seok Pyo, Han Cheol Cho, Ryu Woon Jung, and Tae Hoon Lim, Robot Programming (1st
Eds.), ROBOTIS Co., Ltd. 2017, ISBN 979-11-962307-1-5 .

Reference Book:
1) Learn Robotics Programming: Build and Control AI-enabled Autonomous Robots Using the Raspberry Pi
and Python by Danny Staple, (2nd Eds.), Packt Publishing, 2021
2) Robotics, Vision and Control, Fundamental Algorithms in Python, 3rd edition, Peter Corke, Springer,
2023.

Assessment method: One in-semester and a final examination, and lab assignments. Evaluation scheme: (i)
Second In-Sem Exam: 30%, (ii) End-Sem Exam: 40%, (iii) Labs: 30%.

Course Outcomes: This course will give an opportunity to students to enhance their skills in basics of with a
custom robotic arm and autonomous car (PE1, PE2). Students will gain understanding on 7 degree of freedom
robotic arm, self-drive car, lane assist, cruise control, local navigating, and global navigation which are required for
future automation and transportation. Students will acquire huge skills in Ubuntu, ROS, Python, OpenCV,
TensorFlow, and GitHub (PSO1, PSO2, PSO3, PE3). Today, world leading companies such as Google, TCS,
Amazon, Boston Dynamics, Softbank, DENSO, Disney, HONDA, Hyundai, etc., are looking for the people who
are expertise in computer vision, artificial intelligence and robotics. This program will provide a platform to develop
skills for which companies are interested to hire professionals (PEO3). After successful completion of the course
the student will have the ability to apply engineering knowledge to identify the problem using modern tools for
social development. (PO1, PO2, PO5, PO6). Robotics which is interdisciplinary in nature, having complexity in
development required-long term goal, need to learn ethics to use in society (PO4, PO8, PO12, PE4). Ability to
work in a group project of 3~4students in a team (PO9, PO11, PE5)

Course File IE407 – Internet of Things
Course Title
Internet of Things
Course Code
IE 407
Credit Structure
(L-T-P-Cr)
3-0-2-4
Program and
Semester
B.Tech 6th Semester
Category
ICT Elective
 Instructor
Manish Chaturvedi; Office: FB1-111; Email: manish_chaturvedi@dau.ac.in
Prerequisite
courses
IT305 Computer Networks, EL203  Embedded Hardware Design
Content Outline Internet of Things (IoT) enables embedded smart devices to be an integral part of
the Internet. In this course, we study various aspects of IoT system design
including hardware and software building blocks, communication protocols, and
computation frameworks.
The course begins by discussing a variety of IoT enabled applications identifying
key issues in designing end to end IoT applications. We discuss the preliminaries
of wireless communication and wireless MAC layer protocols.
After the basics, the course focuses on the IoT system specific issues such as
energy  constraints,
 localization,  coverage,  topology  control,  time
synchronization, mobility, routing, data aggregation and dissemination, etc.
Next we look at the building blocks of IoT applications and their characteristics.
A number of domain specific IoT applications and their architecture in vehicular,
social, and sensor networks are discussed. Data analysis tools and machine
learning techniques are introduced to process the large amount of data produced
by IoT applications. The computation models for large scale data processing are
also discussed.
In  the  lab,  we  introduce  the  programming  aspects  of  IoT applications.  All
students are expected to complete a course projects that involves designing and
building a complete IoT application.
A combination of reference books and a set of research papers are used to discuss
the fundamentals, seminal findings and new directions in IoT research.
Text / Reference
Books
•
Sudip Misra,  Anandarup Mukherjee,  Arijit Roy, Introduction to IoT,
Cambridge University  Press.
•
Arshdeep Bahga and Vijay Madisetti, Internet of Things – a hands-on
approach, Universities Press.
•
Waltenegus Dargie and Christian Poellabauer, Fundamentals of Wireless
Sensor Networks – theory and practice, Wiley Publisher
•
C. Siva Ram Murthy and B. S. Manoj, Ad Hoc Wireless Networks:
Architectures and Protocols, Pearson Education
•
Few classic research papers discussed in class

## Page 167

Course Title - AI Literacy, Efficiency, and Ethics
Course Code: IE423
Instructors: V Sunitha and Sanjay Srivastava
Frequency: 2 lectures/Week (90 Minutes each). Lab Assignments to be done in the take-home
mode

1. Course Overview
This course bridges the gap between technical capability and academic utility. The goal is
Metacognition—using AI to "Learn how to Learn," conduct rigorous research, and prepare for
an AI-driven job market.
2. Eligibility and Target Audience
This course is nominally offered for credit as an ICT elective for BTech 6th semester students.
However, this course is also open to BTech 6th/8th semester, MTech, and PhD student for audit.

3. Evaluation Components
(a) In-class assignments / quizzes -
30%
(b) Take-home assignments –
30%
(c) Term Project and viva –
40%

Audit students are expected to perform at the minimum level of a CD grade in (a) and (b) to
receive a PASS grade.

4. Required Resources
Software Stack (Institute Provided)
● Text/Reasoning: ChatGPT Team or Claude (Enterprise privacy).
● Research: Elicit or Consensus (Academic search).
● Data: Julius AI or Tableau (Visualization).
● Accessibility: Otter.ai (Transcription).
Reading List (Selected)
● “The jagged frontier of AI” (Harvard Business School Working Paper).
● “Stochastic Parrots” (Bender et al.).
● “Bloom’s Taxonomy Revisited” (Oregon State University AI Guide).

## Page 168

5. Weekly Detailed Schedule
MODULE I: LITERACY & INCLUSION (The Foundation)
Goal: Moving from "Prompting" to "Algorithmic Thinking."
Week 1: The Paradigm Shift
● Tue (Joint): Predictive vs. Generative AI. The history from LSTMs to Transformers. Why
"Chatting" is the wrong mental model.
● Thu: The Ethics of Assistance. Defining the "Gray Line" between Co-piloting (allowed) and
Plagiarism (banned).
Week 2: Algorithmic Design Thinking
● Tue (Joint): It's not just about prompts; it's about Decomposition. Breaking complex
problems into logic chains.
● Thu: "Logic Mapping." Mapping out a thesis argument's logic tree before asking AI to draft
text.
Week 3: Inclusive Pedagogy (UDL)
● Tue (Joint): AI as the Great Equalizer. How AI supports neurodiversity (Dyslexia, ADHD,
Language barriers).
● Thu: "The Reader Bot." Building a custom "Reader Assistant" that simplifies dense
academic papers into bullet points/analogies.
Week 4: The Hallucination Problem
● Tue (Joint): Probabilistic Reasoning. Why AI lies? The concept of "Temperature" in
models.
● Thu: "Spot the Bot." A gamified session where Faculty and Students compete to identify
AI-generated text vs. human text, analyzing tone and variance.

MODULE II: RESEARCH & DATA FLUENCY (The Workflow)
Goal: Accelerating discovery without compromising rigor.
Week 5: The Literature Review
● Tue (Joint): Synthesis vs. Search. Using Semantic Scholar and Consensus.
● Thu: "The Research Matrix." Generating a comparative analysis table of 10 papers in 15
minutes to find research gaps.

## Page 169

Week 6: Data Storytelling
● Tue (Joint): Visualizing Complexity. Moving beyond static Excel charts to interactive
dashboards.
● Thu: "Thesis Viz." Using AI to suggest 3 alternative ways (Heatmap, Scatter, Sankey) to
visualize their project data.
Week 7: Code & Simulation (Guest: CS Dept)
● Tue (Joint): AI in the Loop. Understanding code generation even if you aren't a coder.
● Thu: "Chat with Data." Using Python libraries (via AI) to clean messy CSV datasets for their
thesis.
Week 8: Mid-Semester Review
● Tue (Joint): "Time-Saved" Showcase. Lightning talks sharing the most efficient workflows
discovered so far.
● Thu: Open Q&A and "Prompt Clinic" (Troubleshooting specific issues).

MODULE III: PEDAGOGY & CAREER (The Divergence)
Goal: Preparing for the future of work and assessment.
Week 9: Assessment Redesign
● Tue (Joint): Bloom’s Taxonomy 2.0. Moving from "Remembering" to "Evaluating."
● Thu: "Exam Prep." Using AI to generate infinite variations of practice problems for
upcoming finals.
Week 10: Personalized Learning
● Tue (Joint): The Age of the Personal Tutor. Adaptive learning systems.
● Thu: "The Super-Tutor." Training an AI to teach a complex concept (e.g., Quantum
Mechanics) via Socratic questioning.
Week 11: The Future of Work
● Tue (Joint): Human-AI Collaboration Models. The "Centaur" vs. the "Cyborg" approach to
work.
● Thu: "Resume Optimization." Beating the Applicant Tracking System (ATS) by optimizing
keywords and formatting.
Week 12: Discipline Deep Dives

## Page 170

● Tue (Joint): The Two Cultures. Bridging STEM and Humanities in AI.
● Thu: "The Shadow Portfolio." Documenting their new AI skills (Prompting, Data Viz) to add
to their LinkedIn/CV.
MODULE IV: ETHICS & POLICY (The Governance)
Goal: Establishing rules and understanding impact.
Week 13: Global Impact
● Tue (Joint): The Hidden Cost. Environmental impact (Water/Energy consumption of
training) and Data Labor ethics.
● Thu: "Debate Club." AI & Intellectual Property: Who owns the output?
Week 14: Capstone Showcase
● Tue (Joint): Final Presentations.
● Thu (Joint): Final Presentations.

6. Certification Requirements
 (AI Literacy Certificate)
To earn the completion certificate, students must submit:
1. The Research Matrix: An AI-generated literature review table.
2. The Career Packet: An AI-optimized Resume & Cover Letter.
3. The Study Log: Evidence of using AI for deep Socratic learning.

## Page 174

Soft Computing (IE422)
Instructor: Pushpendra Kumar
Course Placement: Soft Computing (IE422) is an ICT & Technical Elective / MnC Elective.
Course Format: 3 Lecture hours and 2 Lab hours for the 4-credit course.
Course Prerequisites: Basic knowledge of mathematics fundamentals (including discrete
mathematics), and MATLAB/python.
Course Content: This course is designed for UG students, aiming to build a strong
foundation as well as advanced understanding of Soft Computing techniques. It starts with
the basics of soft computing, its characteristics, and how it differs from hard computing.
The fuzzy logic part covers fuzzy sets, relations, membership functions, fuzzy inference
rules, fuzzification and defuzzification methods along with their applications. The neural
network section introduces artificial neuron models, activation functions, perceptrons,
backpropagation, associative memory, radial basis function networks, and fuzzy neural
networks. The genetic algorithm module explains the GA framework, encoding, selection,
crossover, mutation, optimization problems, convergence theory, and fuzzy logic
controlled genetic algorithms. The course balances theoretical aspects with practical
applications, enabling students to apply these techniques in real-life and engineering
problem-solving. Overall, the content equips learners with essential skills in fuzzy logic,
neural networks, and genetic algorithms, making it relevant for research as well as
practical implementation.
      Text Books:
1.) Timothy J. Ross, Fuzzy Logic with Engineering Applications (John Wiley & Sons,
2009)
2.) S. Haykin, Neural Networks and Learning Machines, third Edition, New Delhi, India:
PHI Learning, 2011.
3.) S. N. Sivanandam and S. N. Deepa, Principles of Soft Computing (John Wiley & Sons,
2007).
4.) D. E. Goldberg, Genetic Algorithms in Search, Optimization and Machine Learning,
Pearson Education, 2006.
      Reference Books:
1.) S. Rajasekaran and G. A. Vijayalakshmi Pai, Neural Networks, Fuzzy Systems
and Evolutionary Algorithms: Synthesis and Applications, second Edition, New
Delhi, India: PHI Learning, 2017.
2.) J. S. R. Jang, C. T. Sun, and E. Mizutani, Neuro-Fuzzy and Soft Computing
(Prentice Hall, 1997).
Assessment Method: The total grade will be based on lab assignments (30%) and scheduled
examinations (two in-semesters and one end-semester) (70%).

## Page 175

Course Outcomes:
This course will enable students to understand the fundamental principles of fuzzy logic,
neural networks, and genetic algorithms, and apply soft computing methods in real-world
applications such as data analysis, control, and decision-making. Learners will gain both
theoretical insights and practical problem-solving skills in soft computing techniques. The
course provides a strong foundation for advanced research or industry roles in artificial
intelligence, machine learning, and computational intelligence. After successful
completion of the course, the student will have the ability to:

CO1: Apply fuzzy logic, neural networks, and genetic algorithms to optimization and
decision-making problems.
CO2: Recognize the feasibility of using soft computing methods for solving real-world and
engineering applications.
CO:3 Demonstrate analytical and computational skills in designing intelligent systems using
soft computing approaches.

LAB TOPICS
1. Introduction to MATLAB/Python for Soft Computing
o Basic operations, visualization, and toolboxes for fuzzy, neural networks, and
optimization.
2. Fuzzy Sets and Membership Functions
o Implement triangular, trapezoidal, Gaussian membership functions and visualize
them.
3. Fuzzy Inference Systems
o Design a simple rule-based fuzzy system (e.g., temperature control, speed
control).
4. Defuzzification Methods
o Implement Centroid, Bisector, MOM, SOM, LOM techniques with examples.
5. Membership value assignments
o Intuition, inference, rank ordering
6. Neural Network Basics
o Implementation of single-layer perceptron and testing with linear separable data.
7. Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) with Backpropagation
o Train an MLP for classification (e.g., XOR problem, digit recognition).
8. Radial Basis Function Networks
o Design and compare with MLP on approximation/classification tasks.
9. Hopfield and BAM neural networks
o Qualitative and quantitative analysis
10. Fuzzy neural networks, Generation of membership functions using NNs
o Qualitative and quantitative analysis
11. Genetic Algorithm (GA) Operators
o Implement encoding, selection, crossover, mutation on a sample problem.
12. GA for Optimization
o Apply GA to solve unconstrained single-objective optimization (e.g., minimizing
a nonlinear function).
13. Integration of all Techniques
o Computing membership functions using genetic algorithms
o Fuzzy Logic Controlled Genetic Algorithms

AbhishekGupta3208,Fac.Bldg.3,Phone:598

OS-OperatingSystems
Course Outline
The course explains the evolution of computer operating systems from a simple program
loader to a complete resource manager of a computing platform. We look at the structure of a
modern operating system, and the design of its components. Important components like
processes, memory, I/O, and file systems are studied in detail. We also study the
implementation of these constructs in the context of a basic Unix-like operating systems.
In the laboratory, experiments/assignments covers: system programming in Unix-like OS,
multithreading, and concurrency control. At the end of the course, students are expected to
have an in-depth understanding of a modern OS architecture and be able to understand and
implement programs using system calls.
Course Outcomes
At the end of the course, students will have the basic understanding of OS architectures,
modules, and the underlying algorithms.They will be able to use system calls to write multi-
threaded applications,and be able to understand and workwithvariousfile systems.They
willbeabletoworkwithOssimulatorsandmeasuretheperformanceofschedulers,call
overheads, and file system comparisons.

IT443 Resampling Techniques and Bayesian Computation (Winter ’2026)
Instructor: Subhasish Basak, 3206, Faculty Block 3
Course Placement: B.Tech. (ICT), Semester-VI
Course Format: 3 Lecture hours + 2 Lab hours (3-0-2)

Course Content: This course provides a rigorous introduction to resampling techniques
and Bayesian computation. The resampling module covers jackknife, bootstrap, and
variance estimation. The Bayesian module covers priors, posteriors, conjugacy, credible
intervals, and MCMC methods including Metropolis–Hastings and Gibbs sampling.

Textbooks:
1. An Introduction to the Bootstrap — Efron & Tibshirani
2. Computer Age Statistical Inference — Efron & Hastie
3. Bayesian Data Analysis — Gelman et al.
4. Monte Carlo Statistical Methods — Robert & Casella

Evaluation Plan:
1. Two Assignments – 20%
2. In-Semester Exam I – 20%
3. In-Semester Exam II – 20%
4. End-Semester Examination – 40%

Course Outcomes:

CO1: Apply fundamental probabilistic concepts, resampling methods, and Bayesian
inference techniques to   model, analyze, and draw conclusions from real-world data.

CO2: Use modern computational tools to design and evaluate solutions for real-world
systems.

## Page 184

CO3: Communicate findings effectively, work in teams, and undertake lifelong learning
by exploring advanced topics.

IT568 – GenAI for Software Engineering
Elective (2-0-4-4), Winter 2026
Instructor: Saurabh Tiwari, 1102, Faculty Block-1
Course Placement: UG/PG
Course format: 2 Lecture hours and 4 Lab hours.
Prerequisite:
 Software Engineering
 Proficiency in at least one programming language (Python/Java/C++).
 Basic understanding of software engineering principles (SDLC, UML,
 testing).
 Familiarity with machine learning concepts is helpful but not mandatory.
Course Content: This course explores how Generative Artificial Intelligence (GenAI) is
transforming the practice of software engineering from requirements analysis and design
modeling to coding, testing, documentation, and maintenance. Students will learn to leverage
large language models (LLMs) and AI-assisted tools to automate and enhance different phases of
the software development lifecycle (SDLC).
By the end of the course, students will design and implement an AI augmented software
engineering assistant that applies GenAI principles to real-world programming and engineering
tasks.
Textbooks:
1. Ian Sommerville, Software Engineering, 10th Edition, Pearson, 2020.
2. Tom M. Mitchell & Emily M. Bender, Generative AI: Concepts, Tools, and Applications,
2023.
3. Humphrey, W. S. (2021). A Discipline for Software Engineering. Addison-
4. Wesley.
5. OpenAI, Anthropic, Hugging Face – Official Documentation and Developer Guides.
6. Selected IEEE/ACM papers on “LLM-assisted Software Engineering.”
Lab Sessions:
Hands-on sessions will expose students to GenAI-powered software engineering tools such as
GitHub Copilot, ChatGPT, Ollama, and, LangGraph, and LangChain. Students will implement AI-
assisted code generation, testing, documentation, and UML modeling pipelines.
 Tools Used: Python, Hugging Face Transformers, LangChain, VS Code, GitHub, Streamlit.
 Computing Resources Required for running the course: Google Colab/Lighting.ai

SC444 Game Theory (Winter 2026)
Instructor: G Venkatesh, Director’s office
Course Placement: B.Tech. (ICT, ICT-CS, MnC), Semester-VI
Course format: 3 Lecture hours per week
What is game theory?
Game theory analyzes situations in which the decisions and actions of two or more individuals (or firms, political
parties, countries) are interdependent. The foundational assumption is that decision-makers are rational and reason
strategically—meaning their reasoning takes into account their knowledge and expectations of other decision-
makers. While originally developed for economics, Game Theory has proven essential for understanding conflict
and cooperation in management, political science, law, biology, and recently, in the design of agentic AI systems.
Methodology:
Delivered through conceptual lectures, supported by Jupyter notebook model demos and in-class game sessions.
 Course Project: This creates hands-on experience in identifying game-like problem situations, creating an
evolutionary game model for them, and using the model to devise remedial measures.
 Logistics: Students must enrol in Google Classroom to access course materials and instructions. All
communication will occur via this platform.
 Technical Workflow: Project submissions must be Jupyter notebooks coded in Python and executed using
Google Colab within the Google Classroom/Drive environment.
Prerequisites:
 Essential: Comfort with logical reasoning, basic probability, and coding in Python.
 Desirable: Some background in intermediate microeconomics.
Evaluation plan:
Grading will be calculated based on the following components:
 Group Project: 30%
 Quizzes: 30% (Based on the best 3 out of 4 quizzes)
 End-term Exam: 40%
Course Outcomes:
This course aims to provide a systematic introduction to the tools of game theory and its applications. By the end of
the course, students will be able to create mathematical and computational models to understand:
 Market Dynamics: Competition among firms, Duopolies and oligopolies
 Strategic Behaviour: Credible deterrence strategies, Benefits of being unpredictable, Negotiation and
bargaining, Strategic voting.
 Information Economics: Eliciting information, Principal-agent games, Moral hazard in insurance,
Signaling games and advertising,  Adverse selection and the use of warranties.
 Mechanism Design: Incentive design, Auction design, Product bundling.
 Evolutionary Models: The evolution of cooperation and free-rider elimination.
Students will demonstrate these outcomes by developing their own evolutionary game models for specific situations,
documented as Jupyter notebooks.
Text Books:
“A primer in game theory” by Robert Gibbons, Pearson India, 2018.
“A Course In Game Theory” by Martin Osborne and Ariel Rubinstein, MIT Press, 1994
https://sites.math.rutgers.edu/~zeilberg/EM20/OsborneRubinsteinMasterpiece.pdf
Reference Books:
“An introduction to Game Theory” by Martin Osborne, Oxford University Press, 2012
“Game Theory” by Giacomo Bonanno, https://faculty.econ.ucdavis.edu/faculty/bonanno/PDF/GT_book.pdf
“Fun and games: A text on Game theory” by Ken Binmore, (Indian ed) AITBS Publishers, New Delhi, 1999.
“Game theory with economic applications” by H S Bierman and L Fernandez, (Indian ed), Pearson education, 1998.
“Games and information”  by Eric Rasmusen, Basil Blackwell ltd. 1989.
“Strategies and Games: Theory and Practice” by Prajit K Dutta, MIT Press.
