Introduction to Financial Mathematics (Winter 2026)
Instructor: Dr. Abhishek Tilva
Course Code: SCXXX
Program / Semester: B.Tech ICT, 6th Semester
Category: Science Elective
Credit Structure:
• Lecture hours per week: 3
• Tutorial hours per week: 1
• Practical hours per week: 0
• Total Credits: 4
Prerequisite Courses: Calculus, Probability, Linear Algebra
Course Abstract
This course introduces fundamental concepts of financial mathematics, including discrete-time models
for pricing and hedging of financial derivatives, arbitrage-free valuation, and basic portfolio theory.
The course then develops continuous-time models based on stochastic calculus, leading to the Black–
Scholes–Merton framework.
Course Objectives
The objectives of this course are:
• To provide students with a solid foundation in the mathematical principles underlying financial
engineering.
• To equip students with tools for modeling financial instruments in both discrete and continuous
time.
• To develop the ability to apply stochastic methods in asset pricing.
Evaluation / Grading Policy
• Attendance: 10% (% attendance ÷ 10)
• In-semester Exam I: 25%
• In-semester Exam II: 25%
• Final Exam: 40%

## Page 192

SC463: Quantum Computation
Course content
DA-IICT, B.Tech,
Objective:
This course is an introduction to the emerging field of Quantum Computers. Students are
expected to know Quantum Mechanics but it is not mandatory. The objective of this course
is to introduce the students to a different kind of logical system, namely, the quantum logic,
based on the superposition of the states 0 and 1. The phenomenon of entangled states and
its use in controlled operations are introduced. With this basic tools from the physics of
Quantum Mechanics the course intends to prepare the students to understand some of the
important algorithms in quantum computation, quantum Information and Cryptography.
Prerequisite: Quantum Mechanics, Linear Algebra.
1. Introduction to Quantum Mechanics
(i) Postulates of Quantum Mechanics.
(ii) Measurement in Quantum Mechanics.
(iii) The bra-ket and density operator formalism.
(iv) Composite (Multi qubit) System.
2. Introduction to Computer Science.
(i) Turing Machine.
(ii) Circuit model of computation.
(iii) Measures of complexity.
(iv) Energy for computation.
(v) Reversible gates.
3. Quantum Circuits.
(i) Qubits, Quantum Gates.
(ii) Controlled Operations.
(iii) Unitarity of gates.
(iv) Relation to reversibility.
4. Quantum Algorithms.
(i) Quantum Fourier Transforms.
(ii) Order finding and factoring.
(iii) Quantum Search Algorithms.
(iv) Quantum Cryptography.
5. Quantum Noise
1

## Page 193

(i) Quantum operations.
(ii) Quantum Error Correction
Outcome:
The students learn the idea of qubits which implements parallel processing in quantum
mechanics. The introduction of gates as Unitary operators acting on qubits help them to look
at quantum computers as intrinsically reversible machines. The student learns a completely
new algebra of logical system based on subspaces of a vector space as opposed to the boolean
algebra based on subsets of a set. Controlled gates provides a concrete application of the
complex phenomena of entanglement and measurement in quantum mechanics. The students
learn the Deutsch’s Algorithm, the search algorithm and algorithms based on Quantum
Fourier Transform.
This includes Shor’s probabilistic algorithm for factoring an integer.
The students learn the interesting application of generating a private key between parties
using quantum phenomenon, rather than transmitting a predetermined key, in Quantum
Cryptography. The course prepares the student to do further reading or get into research in
the exciting field of Quantum Computation and Quantum Information.

## Page 196

SC301 Numerical Linear Algebra
Aditya Tatu
1. Credits: 3-0-0-3
2. Prerequisites: Linear Algebra.
3. Summary: A first course in Linear algebra equips students with a set of concepts and
tools to handle finite dimensional vector spaces. These are heavily utilized in a wide
range of applications in different fields like Image and Signal Processing, Machine
Learning, Data Science, Computer Graphics, Mechanical Engineering etc. This course
studies algorithms for some core Linear algebra concepts that have been incorporated
into libraries like LAPACK, for example, Solving Linear equations and Matrix factor-
izations like QR , Cholesky and SVD. There are direct and iterative methods for many
of these tools.
These problems in some instances like those encountered in Data Science may involve
large matrices which makes it important to study the complexity and stability of these
algorithms. The course will examine a wide variety of numerical algorithms, will
study them from the perspectives of accuracy and stability, and will discuss a few
applications depending on the available time.
4. Content: (not necessarily in this order).
(a) Review of LA: Vector Spaces: Rn and Cn, Matrices and Vectors, Linear indepen-
dence, Rank of a matrix, Subspaces, Eigenvalues and Eigenvectors. Inner prod-
ucts and Norms in Rn and Cn, Orthogonality, Matrix Norms, Spectral Radius.
(b) Review of Number representation: Integer and Floating point representation,
Absolute and relative errors.
(c) Linear Equations: Applications: Numerical Solution of PDEs, Curve fitting (spe-
cial case of Least squares), Gaussian elimination, LU decomposition, Partial and
Full pivoting, Stability and efficiency – Condition number. Special cases – Tridi-
agonal systems, SPD matrices. Iterative Methods: Order of Convergence, Jacobi,
Gauss-Seidel, Successive over-relaxation(SOR), Steepest Descent, Conjugate Gra-
dient. Preconditioning.
(d) Matrix Decomposition - QR and Cholesky: Applications – Computing determi-
nant, Orthonormal basis from a basis, Methods: Gram Schmidt Orthogonaliza-
tion, Givens rotations, Householder reflections. Cholesky Decomposition.
(e) Eigendecomposition: Symmetric and Unsymmetric Eigenvalue problem. Ap-
plications – Difference and Differential equations, Method: Power iteration, QR
iterations, Hessenberg and Schur forms.
(f) SVD: (if time permits) - SVD – Existence, Interpretation, Applications – Least
squares, PCA.
1

5. References:
(a) Matrix Computations, Golub and Van Loan, John Hopkins Press, 4th ed., 2013
(b) Numerical Linear Algebra with Applications, William Ford, Academic Press, 2015.
(c) Numerical Linear Algebra, Trefethen and Bau III, SIAM, 1997.
6. Evaluation: (Assuming ≤50 students)

(e) The student will be able to understand the Symmetric and Assymetric eigenvalue
problem and their applications.

## Page 198

SC475 – Time Series Analysis
Course Placement: Time Series Analysis is an elective course offered to third and fourth-year students of
the B.Tech  program.

Course format:  3-hour lecture every week

Prerequisites: A basic course in statistics and probability, Linear Algebra, Calculus, and either Nonlinear
Dynamics or Modelling and Simulation. Python, R or Matlab programming skills.

Course content: This course introduces students to the essential tools and concepts in time series analysis,
covering both time and frequency domains. Time series typically arise in many processes that require
consecutive measurements of quantities. Understanding the basic structure and information of a time series
requires concepts often used in signal and image processing, statistics, dynamical systems, and statistical
physics. The course aims to develop a working foundation at the undergraduate level that enables systematic
analysis of time series arising in various disciplines. The course primarily focuses on traditional statistical
methods used in time series analysis.  In addition, it aims to expose students to modern challenges and
selected nonlinear methods that are used extensively in the research literature.  We will primarily focus on
EEG and financial time series to develop a more hands-on understanding of the concepts discussed in class.
A single textbook is insufficient for this course. Some of the relevant resources that will be referred to
are:
Reference Books:

George E. P. Box, Gwylim M. Jenkins, Gregory C. Reinsel, Greta M. Ljung, Time Series Analysis.
Forecasting and Control, Fifth Edition, Wiley, 2016
 Donald B. Percival, Andrew T. Walden, Wavelet Methods for Time Series Analysis, Cambridge
University Press, 2000
 Holger Kantz, Thomas Schreiber, Nonlinear Time Series Analysis, Springer, 1999
 R. H. Shumway and D. S. Stoffer, Time Series Analysis and Its Applications: With R Examples,
fourth edition. Springer 2017.
 C. Chatfield, and H. Xing, The Analysis of Time Series. An Introduction with R, seventh edition.
Chapman & Hall/CRC  2019.
 Analyzing Neural time series data, Theory and Practice, Mike X Cohen, MIT press, 2014.
 Analysis of Financial time series, R. S. Shay, Wiley, 2010.
 Econophysics: An introduction, Sitabhra Sinha, Arnab Chatterjee, Anirban Chakraborti, and
Bikas K. Chakraborti, Wiley 2010.
 An introduction to Econophysics, Correlations and Complexity in Finance, R. N. Mantegna and
H. E. Stanley, Cambridge, 2004.

After completing the course, the students would be able to analyze time series using variety of methods. In

## Page 201

SC402 – Introduction to Cryptography (3-0-0-3)

Instructor: Dr. Manik Lal Das
Office: 2103, Faculty Block 2
Email: maniklal_das@daiict.ac.in

Course Outlines
Cryptography is concerned with the mathematical, algorithmic, and implementation aspects of
information and network security. The topics in this course include:

 Classical cryptography vs. Modern cryptography - affine cipher, vigenere cipher; Security
definition and adversarial assumptions
 Secret Key Cryptography (SKC): SPN, pseudo-random functions; Modular Arithmetic-1;
Block ciphers; Stream ciphers; Modes of operation; Security notion – perfect security,
semantic security, provable security, indistinguishability based security, CPA, CCA.
 Hash functions, Pseudo-Random Generators – Properties, Constructions; Message
Authentication Codes; Security notion of MAC; Authenticated Encryptions.
 Public Key Cryptography (PKC):  Modular Arithmetic-2, RSA, DSA, El Gamal scheme;
PKI, Key Management; Security notion – IND-CPA, IND-CKA.
 Advanced Topics and Applications - Secret sharing scheme; Secure Multiparty
Computation; Identity-based cryptography.

Reading Materials
 Introduction to Modern Cryptography - Jonathan Katz and Yehuda Lindell, CRC
Press.
 Cryptography: Theory and Practice - Douglas Stinson, Third edition, CRC Press.
 Handbook of Applied Cryptography - Alfred Menezes, Paul Oorschot and Scott Vanstone.
Available Online .

Grading Policy:
 Assignments, Class participation – 20%
 In-semester examination (one) – 30%
 End semester examination – 50%

Course credits: LTP-C (3-0-2 -4)
Prerequisites:

Image Processing OR computer vision

Machine Learning

Python
Course Outcome:
After the successful completion of the course the students should:
i.
develop an in-depth understanding of the biometric recognition systems and all associated
aspects;
ii. be able to comprehend and present state-of-the-art research papers;
iii. be able to work on a specific research problem as part of the project work and,
iv. be able to write technical report
Course content:
Introduction to biometrics: basic processing pipeline, biometric recognition including identification and
verification, biometric traits or modalities, specific characteristics of biometric traits, different biometric
modes: face, speech, iris, palmprint, finger-vein, palm-vein, fingerprint. Evaluation metrics: impostor
scores, genuine scores, FAR, FRR, EER, GAR, DET curve, CMC curve, Receiver operating characteristics,
Face recognition: state-of-the-art in face biometrics, deep learning architecture pipeline, hand-crafted
features, vascular biometrics: Acquisition, vascular biometric traits: palm-vein, finger-vein, dorsal hand-
vein, iris. Attacks: Presentation attacks on biometric systems: real-world attack instances, different defense
measures, Adversarial attacks using physical accessories, morphing attacks, multi-modal biometric
systems: fusion at different levels: image-level fusion, score-level fusion, and decision-level fusion.
Cancelable biometrics, secure and privacy preserving biometrics, face de-identification, cross-domain/
heterogeneous recognition, Person re-identification.

Course Description:
Deep Learning aims to equip students with a comprehensive understanding of the principles,
algorithms, and applications of neural networks and deep learning techniques. Through
theoretical knowledge and practical experience, students will gain the expertise to effectively
design, implement, and deploy neural network-based solutions across various domains. The
course emphasizes practical application development through course projects and lab sessions,
preparing
students
to
understand
and
implement deep learning algorithms and
methodologies.

Suggested Books:
●​ Deep Learning by Ian Goodfellow, Yoshua Bengio, Aaron Courville, The MIT  Press
[2016].
●​ Deep Learning with Python by François Chollet, Manning Publications, 2nd Edition
[2021].
●​ Deep Learning from Scratch by Seth Weidman, O'Reilly, 1st Edition [2019]
Course Outcomes:
●​ Understand the advantages of deep learning.
●​ Gain in-depth knowledge of advanced architectures, including CNNs, RNNs, LSTMs,
GRUs, Attention mechanisms, Transformers, and Autoencoders (at a high level).
●​ Theoretical and practical understanding of the architectures and insights into design
choices for better model development.

## Page 211

IT549: Deep Learning
Academic Year 2025-26 (Winter)

Challenges and Course Project Viva [20% + 25% = 45%]
The following points regarding the course project are worth noting.

Student Groups (Just for Challenges)
●​ Students will be asked to form groups of two members. Each group will be involved in
100-minute ML development challenges.
○​ 45% of your marks will be based on team efforts, so choose your members
wisely.
○​ Teams will remain unchanged throughout the semester once registered. No
requests for changes will be entertained.
●​ Team registration will open the second week after classes start, and you must register
your team via a Google form within three days of the announcement.
●​ During lab hours, there will be three machine-learning challenges. The three most
proficient teams shall be acknowledged with a bonus of up to 3% on their respective
scores.
Course Project [25%]
●​ There is only one course project: an End-to-End ML application (full-stack, i.e.,
front-end and Back-end, build and deploy) that solves a data-driven problem with high
social impact.
●​ Each group must select a thematic domain: Finance, E-commerce, Healthcare, Pharma,
Sports, Entertainment, Renewable Energy, Oil & Gas, Automobile, Agriculture, FMCG,
Cybersecurity, Social Media, Supply Chain, Manufacturing, Business Automation, or any
other exciting and valuable socially aligned domain.
●​ Each group will define the problem in their selected domain and collect dataset(s) from
reliable sources, including publicly available ones (no two groups can work on the
same dataset). You are encouraged to gather additional data to enhance your dataset
and better address the problem.
●​ Each group must add a novel contribution to their project and compare their work with
the existing baselines.
●​ The project guideline document will provide information on domain allocation, general
instructions, evaluation criteria, and other protocols.

## Page 213

Specification and verification of systems (IT-449)

Course placement: It is a technical elective as well as MnC elective.
Course format: 3-0-0-3
Course contents: Modelling hardware and software systems through transition
systems; modelling concurrency and interleaving in systems; linear time
properties; safety properties; liveness properties; Buchi automata, omega
regular properties; syntax and semantics of LTL (linear temporal logic); syntax
and semantics of CTL (computation tree logic); introduction to model checking
tool SPIN.
Selected readings: (1) Principles of model-checking, MIT Press, Joost Pieter
Katoen, Christel Baier. (2) Logic in computer sciences, modelling and reasoning
about systems, Cambridge press, Michael Huth, Mark Ryan.
Course outcomes:  Designing a software or a hardware system is a nontrivial
task. The design of the system is formally modelled and then verified to gain
confidence. Model checking is one of the predominant techniques for
verification of systems.  This course aims to introduce students about the
mathematical and logical foundations of model checking.

Distributed Database Systems IT 504 (3‐0‐2‐4) 
MTech ICT‐SS Core Elective  
Prerequisites:  Fundamental Course on DBMS 
Instructor: Minal Bhise  minal_bhise@dau.ac.in 
Objectives:  
Collection of correlated databases distributed over a cluster of servers can facilitate scalable query processing. 
Course includes foundational work, recent developments and trends in scalable database management systems. 
Modern Data Storage and Query processing for Parallel and distributed databases will be discussed. Students will 
be working on research projects in the domain of modern distributed data storage and query processing during 
labs. Students will be using various SQL and NoSQL database management tools for implementing their research 
ideas. 
Resources: 

Tamer Oszu and Patrick Valduriez, Principles of Distributed Systems, Fourth Edition, Springer 2020 

Silberschatz, Korth & Sudarshan, Database System Concepts, Seventh Edition, 2019,McGraw‐Hill 

Research Papers 
Course Outcomes: 
The course will make students understand various fundamental concepts on which Distributed Databases work. 
The students will have some hands‐on experience with distributed database technology and tools through a series 
of assignments and a project. They will also learn some NoSQL technologies like Graph tools.

## Page 217

IT 584 : Approximation Algorithms

Course Placement : Approximation Algorithms is an elective course for the first year (2nd
Semester) students of the  M.Tech program with software systems specialization. It is also open
as an elective to  B.Tech and B.Tech MnC students (recommended for those interested in
theoretical analysis, mathematical and algorithmic aspects of CS).

Course Format: It is  3 hours of lecture and 1 hour of tutorial every week.

Credit Structure: 3-1-0-4 (L-T-P-C)

Course Content: Many problems in computer science are known to be “hard”. It is
computationally very expensive to solve these exactly. The aim of this course is to learn various
techniques to solve such problems in an approximate manner. The course will discuss
algorithmic techniques like greedy algorithms, local search,  approximation schemes, LP based
algorithms, randomized algorithms and a few others. For each technique the course will cover
one or more problems and also focus on detailed analysis of the approximation obtained.

Prerequisites: A course on basic algorithms and data structures, discrete mathematics.
Knowledge of probability and random variables, linear algebra  and optimization will be useful.
Mathematical maturity and interest will help appreciate and profit from the course.

Reference Books:
There is no prescribed textbook. We will follow material from different sources. Few good
reference books are:
●​ Approximation Algorithms by V. Vazirani
●​ The Design of Approximation Algorithms by David Williamson and David Shmoys
●​ Randomized Algorithms by Rajeev Motwani
●​ Probability and Computing Mitzenmacher and Upfal

Assessment Method : (Subject to modifications)
Term Paper/Flip class - 30%
Mid Sem- 30%
End Sem - 40%

## Page 218

Course Outcomes:
After successful completion of this course, students will be able to:
●​ Understand the notion of hard problems  and need of approximation algorithms
●​ Understand techniques to design approximation algorithms for various problems
●​ Analyze approximation algorithms for different problems

## Page 219

Advanced Statistical Tools for Data Science
Course Placement: It’s an elective for B Tech ICT, B Tech Maths and Computing, Masters in Data
Science, M Tech, Ph.D.
Prerequisite:  A basic course in probability & statistics
Course format (3-1-0-4): Three hours’ lecture and one-hour tutorial every week.
Course Content:

Modelling data using regression: Regression problem, Regression models: parametric & non-parametric,
Model selection, Model estimation & Inference, Multilevel/Hierarchical regression models

Resampling Techniques- Jackknife & Bootstrap: Estimation of Bias and Variance, Resampling based
inference (estimation, testing and prediction)

Bayesian Data Analysis: Frequentist versus Bayesian Inference, Prior & posterior distributions, Credible
intervals, Bayes’ factor

Missing Data Analysis: Different missingness mechanisms, Different Imputation methods, EM Algorithm,

Causal Inference: Causation versus correlation, Potential outcome framework for causal inference,
Causal inference using data from (i) randomized experiments, (ii) observational studies, (iii) natural
experiments.
References:
1.  Data analysis using regression and multilevel hierarchical models – Andrew
Gelman and Jennifer Hill (Cambridge university press)
2.  An introduction to the bootstrap — Bradley Efron and Robert Tibshirani (CRC Press)
3.  The jackknife, the bootstrap and other resampling plans—Bradley Efron (CBMS-NSF
Regional Conference Series)
4.  Bayesian data analysis—Andrew Gelman, John B. Carlin, Hal H. Stern, David B. Dunson,
Aki Vehtari, Donald B, Rubin (Chapman Hall/CRC Press)
5. Statistical Analysis with Missing Data: Roderick J. A. Little and Donald Rubin (Wiley
Interscience)

6. Multiple imputation for non-response in surveys: Donald Rubin (John Wiley)

7. The book of why: Judea Pearl and Dana Mackenzie (Basic Books)

8. Observation and experiment: Paul R. Rosenbaum (Harvard University Press)

9. Counterfactuals and causal inference: Stephen L Morgan and Christopher Winship (Cambridge
University Press)

10. Causal Inference for statistics, social and biomedical sciences: Guido W. Imbens and Donald
B. Rubin (Cambridge University Press)

Course Title

Quantum Machine Leaning (QML)

Course Code
IT401
Credit Structure
3-0-2-4 (L-T-P-C)
Category
Elective
Semester: II/VI
Winter Semester (AY 25-26)
Program
MTech/BTech/PhD
Prerequisites
Basics in Machine Learning (ML) and/or Deep Learning (DL),  Linear Algebra
Course
Objectives/
Brief Course
Description
In recent years, machine learning has expanded into virtually every
domain—from military and aerospace to agriculture, manufacturing, finance,
and healthcare. As ML models continue to grow in complexity, often
containing billions of parameters, training them efficiently has become
increasingly challenging. Quantum computing offers a promising path
forward by addressing problems that are intractable for classical computing
systems. Its ability to manipulate and compute over multiple Quantum bits
(qubits) simultaneously enables a form of massive parallelism grounded in
the principles of superposition, entanglement, and interference. Unlike
classical systems that rely on sequential information processing, quantum
computers leverage these quantum mechanical properties to accelerate
computation in fundamentally new ways.
This course introduces students to the foundational concepts of quantum
computing and explores how these ideas can be applied to machine learning
and deep learning. Through IBM’s Qiskit (an open source SDK) tool and
hands-on course projects on ML/DL, students gain experience in
implementing supervised and unsupervised ML/DL algorithms using
quantum and hybrid quantum–classical approaches.

Evaluation/
Grading Policy
● Class participation - 10%
● Midsem1 exam -20%
● Midsem2 exam -20%
● Lab and course project: - 50%.
Course
Materials/
References
1. Quantum machine learning for data scientists by Dawid Kopczyk in
arXiv, Quantum Physics, 2018
2. Quantum machine learning, What quantum computing means to
data mining, book by Peter Wittek, Elsevier, 2014
3. Quantum Machine Learning with Python, book by Santanu
Pattanayak, researcher Qualcomm corp, Apress, 2021
4. Quantum machine learning: A classical perspective, Proceedings
Royal Society, A474, 2017
5. Quantum machine learning MOOC, created by Peter Wittek from
University of Toronto, 2019 (video lectures)
6. Introduction to Quantum computing, NPTEL (video lectures)

## Page 224

7. Quantum machine learning, Qiskit Global Summer school, 2021
(video lectures)

Detailed Course
Content

Difference between classical and quantum machine learning,
Brief introduction to quantum applications, vector spaces, tensor products and
qubits,
Superposition, entanglement, interference, quantum gates,
Quantum circuits, density matrix,
Variational quantum circuit
Grover search algorithm,
Quantum Fourier transform
Quantum encoding (basis, amplitude, angle, higher order encoding), quantum
measurement,
Quantum Eigen solver, quantum approximation optimization algorithm (QAOA)
Variational quantum algorithms, noisy intermediate scale quantum (NISQ)
computing,
Quantum support vector machine
Quantum k-means clustering
Quantum neural networks (QNN).
Quantum classical hybrid classification
quantum classical hybrid time series analysis
Quantum classical hybrid super-resolution.

Course Outcome:

CO1: Understand the basics in quantum computing and machine learning
CO2: Analyse the quantum circuits operations
CO3: Identify problems in machine learning to solve by using quantum approach
CO4: Test the algorithms in labs and carry out projects using QISKIT.

## Page 226

IT 507—Advanced Image Processing (3-0-2-4)
AY: 2025-2026 (Winter)

Course Placement:  Advanced image processing is an elective course for UG and PG
students.
Course Format: It is 3 hours’ lecture and 2 hours’ lab every week.
Course Objective:
The objective of this course is to introduce some basic to advanced levels of techniques for
processing image. The techniques include various spatial domain, transformed domain
techniques to enhance, to restore, and to segment image for higher level processing.

Course Content:
1) Basics of digital image – Basic steps of processing, Sampling and quantization, Pixels
relationship
2) Intensity transformations – Log, Gamma, Histogram equalization, specification,
Advanced application
3) Spatial domain filtering – Correlation, Convolution, Filtering for image smoothing
and sharpening, Advanced spatial domain filters
4) Frequency domain filtering – Impulse and its properties, Fourier series, Fourier
Transform of one variable, two variables (continuous and discrete), Aliasing, signal
reconstruction from sampled data, DFT, Frequency domain filters and its operation
5) Image Restoration -- Mathematical model of degradation, Noise models, Linear
position-invariant degradation, Estimating the degradation function, Inverse filtering,
Wiener filtering, Constraint least square filtering. Advanced applications
6) Image Segmentation -- Point, line and edge detection, Hough Transformation,
Thresholding, Region-based segmentation, Motion-based segmentation. Advanced
Techniques
7) Representation and Description -- Representation, Boundary descriptors, regional
descriptors, Principal components for description, SIFT.
8) Object Recognition: Bayes Classifier, Neural Network based Classifier, CNN-based
classifier

## Page 227

Readings:
1. R. C. Gonzalez, R. E. Woods, “Digital Image Processing”, 3rd Edition, Pearson
Prentice Hall, 2008.
2. M. Sonka, V. Hlavac, R. Boyel, “Image Processing Analysis, and Machine Vision”,
2nd Edition, PWS Publishing, 1999.
3. S. E. Umbaugh, “Digital Image Processing and Analysis”, 2nd Edition, CRC Press,
2010.
4. A. L. Bovik, “The Essential Guide to Image Processing”, Academic Press, 2009.
5. Research Articles (suggested by the instructors)

Course Outcomes:
After completion of this course, students should be able to:
 Understand the methods of processing an image for a particular application. [P1]
 Identify the reason of image degradation, and formulate the model to address the
issue. [P2]
 Get ideas to develop new algorithms for processing images. [P3, P4, P5]
 Analyze the shortcomings of existing methods, and ponder upon those. [P2, P5]
 Work in a group for a project, and present their work to engineering community [P9,
P10].

## Page 230

IT567 – Reinforcement Learning
1. Credit Structure (L-T-P-Cr): 3-0-2-4
2. Course Code: TBD
3. Program/Semester: Btech (Sem VI - MnC, ICT & ICT-CS); Mtech (SEM II - ICT-ML)
4. Category:  Technical elective (TE) /Specialization elective (I/II)
5. Prerequisites: Probability, statistics & information theory/Probability & random processes
6. Foundation for: B. Tech. projects, M. Tech. major projects and M. Tech. thesis.
7. Abstract Content:
Reinforcement learning (RL) is one of the paradigms of machine learning which delves on the
concept of learning by maintaining  a balance between  exploration  and exploitation.  It is
different from supervised and unsupervised learning, taught in the courses on machine learning.
This course on RL aims to equip students with a computational approach to learning through
interaction. Specifically, the students will be introduced to goal-directed learning and  trial-and-
error search with delayed reward. The notions and definitions of policy and policy function,
value function and reward will be introduced. Once the theoretical formulation is done, Monte-
carlo methods will be introduced for possible computation of the strategies based on RL.
Students will learn the applications of concepts learned in the course on probability theory,
stochastic processes and optimization in solving problems in the realm of RL.
The laboratory problems for the course will try to enhance the understanding of the concepts
through several hands-on exercises. A few of the lab sessions will be reserved for case studies
including games such as Tic-Tac-Toe, game of go, etc., finance, image processing and digital
communication.

8. Suggested Text/s and other resources:
[1]. Reinforcement learning: An Introduction by R. S. Sutton and A. G. Barto, 2nd edition, MIT
press.
[2]. Reinforcement Learning: State-of-the-Art, M. Wiering and M. van Otterlo, Springer.
[3]. Reinforcement Learning and Stochastic Optimization: A Unified Framework for Sequential
Decisions by W. Powell, Wiley.
[4]. Reinforcement Learning and Optimal Control by D. Bertsekas, Athena Scientific
[5]. Deep Reinforcement Learning Hands-On, M. Lapan, Packt publication
[6]. Distributional Reinforcement Learning by M. Bellemare et. al. MIT press.
[7]. Deep Reinforcement Learning in Action by Zai and Brown, Manning Public.
[8]. David Silver's course on Reinforcement Learning, available at
https://www.davidsilver.uk/teaching/

## Page 231

[9]. Balaraman Ravindran's course on Reinforcement Learning, available at
https://nptel.ac.in/courses/106106143

## Page 233

Software Project Management (IT414)
Course placement: Software Project Management is an elective course for the
final year B.Tech, second year M.Tech, MScIT and MScDS programs.
Course format: 3 hours’ lecture and 2-hour laboratory every week.
Course content:
Practical use of software project management requires understanding of core
software design principles, related managerial aspects, an influx of new ideas,
and  the  courage  to  improve  traditional  approaches  for  handling  evolving
scenario of IT industry. As a software engineer and an early career manager, you
need to know the methods, workflows and tools to handle continuously growing
complexity and shortened project management cycles. You must be able to work
in teams to build high-quality software. In this course, we will introduce the basic
concepts  of  software  project  management  along  with  some  contemporary
industry  practices.  You  will  also  get  a  hands-on  on  project  documentation,
feasibility  analysis,  risk  handling  techniques,  etc.  that  are  used  when
implementing complex software projects through different case studies.
Books and Literature:

Walker Royce. Software Project Management: A Unified Framework.

Kathy Schwalbe. Information Technology Project Management.

Stellman & Greene. Applied Software Project Management.

Richard Thayer, Edward Yourdon. Software Engineering Project
Management.

Bob Hughes and Mike Cotterell. Software Project Management.

Steve McConnell. Code Complete: A Practical Handbook of Software
Construction.

M. Lewrick, P. Link, and L. Leifer. The Design Thinking Playbook.
Assessment Method: 6-8 lab assignments, a group project,  class tests,  and
end-semester examination.
Course Outcomes:
Software  Project  Management  theory  introduces  students  to  fundamental
principles  of  software  project  management as  well  as  risk  management
principles along with some related activities carried out in large scale IT software
projects.  It  also  covers  key  topics  including  estimation,  project  planning,
scheduling,  project  reviews,  process  improvement,  etc.  activities  involved  in
managing software projects. This course also address topics related to handling
outsourced projects, project failures etc. This course also prepares students for
higher level specialization in software project management courses.
After successful completion of the course the student will have the ability to,

Understand, analyze, plan, manage, and implement software projects (P1,
P2, P12).

## Page 234


Apply project management principles for implementation / handling
software projects using various tools and techniques (P3, P5, P11).

Plan, monitor, track, and review software projects in a large group setup
(P9, P11).

Create software project management documents, detailed project plans,
schedules, along with necessary software implementation strategy. (P10,
P11)

8. Abstract Content
This course aims to develop a systematic understanding of the forecasting
models from machine learning perspective. The course offers a smooth journey
from basic Least Square Regression model developed around year 1805 to
modern complex forecasting methods after 2020. The content of the course
covers the variants of point and probabilistic forecasting methods with detailed
statistical and mathematical analysis for panel and time-series data.

## Page 238

Course Outline
Course Title- Approaches to Globalization
Course Code- HM 467
Semester- Winter
Category- Open Elective
Instructors- Prof. Madhumita Mazumdar
Course Objectives
This course will introduce students to the economic social and cultural
phenomena of globalization. Based on the understanding of the implicit
relationship of productive processes to society and history, the course
will present to students a series of cases, mainly drawn from Asia to
understand the multiple sites and forms of globalization.
Unit One will introduce students to the concepts, definitions and
historical origins of the processes of globalization in the 20th century.
Unit two will focus on the complex travel of information, goods,
technologies, ideologies and people around the world – and how these
are apprehended and negotiated by people in specific locations.
Unit three will draw attention to the argument that while globalisation is
a formidable force for change, there are also equally formidable factors
that resist it.

## Page 239

Unit four will Globalisation will introduce students to debates on
migration,technology, media and the environment.
Students will have the opportunity to investigate a relevant topic of the
choice and apply social-scientific explanatory frameworks in a final
paper.

Course Outcomes
The intensive reading and analysis that the course expects from students,
is to make them critically engage with issues of social and economic
“change” they see around them. At the end of the course they will be
expected to develop a deeper understanding of specific questions
pertaining to globalization and social change. Is change always a desired
and set goal? Does it follow any standardized or uniform agenda? Is
globalization an innate drive towards socio-cultural homogenization ?
Or does globalization invariably lead to cultural and social
fragmentation? And finally what is the defining role of communication
technology in both driving the processes of globalization and generating
resistance to it?
Course Structure
A sequence of themed lectures will cover some essential ideas that the
course intends to impart to you. Related to the lectures will be readings

## Page 240

that would help you to understand the basic ideas. The lectures are not to
simplify the readings but to approach the readings with a degree of
critical attention- particularly the ones that are portioned as discussion
readings. Consequently each unit of study will have a lecture or two and
set of case studies. Student groups would develop presentations based on
the readings and research conducted at both the individual and group
level.
Course Topics to be considered
UNIT 1. INTRODUCTION; MAKING SENSE OF GLOBALIZATION
UNIT 2. CAPITALISM AND NEOLIBERALISM
Unit 3. HISTORICAL AND ANTHROPOLOGICAL FRAMEWORKS
UNIT 4.THE ANTECEDENTS OF GLOBALIZATION-
COLONIALISM AND
IMPERIALISM
UNIT 5. THE REVOLUTION IN COMMUNICATION
TECHNOLOGIES
UNIT 6. TRANSNATIONALISM AND HUMAN FLOWS
UNIT 7. THE GLOBAL FLOWS OF CULTURE AS PLEASURE AND
AS PRODUCT

## Page 241

UNIT 8.COSMOPOLITANISM AND LOCALISM: CULTURE AND
RESISTANCE
UNIT 9. AGAINST GLOBALIZATION
UNIT10. GLOBALIZATION- FROM “BUZZWORD TO CURSE”

Course Evaluation : Active and informed participation in the class will
count as 10% of the final grade. An end- semester exam would count for
50% of the final grade and a group research project based on the themes
and readings presented will constitute 40% of the final grade.
Select Readings
Appadurai.A, Modernity at Large: Cultural Dimensions of Globalisation
Appadurai A (ed). Globalisation, Durham, NC: Duke University Press.
Bauman, Zygmunt. Globalization: the human consequences. Cambridge:
Polity Press, 1998.
Beck, U. Risk society. London: Sage, 1992.
Castells, Manual. The informational city. Oxford: Blackwell, 1989.
Castells, Manual. The rise of the network society, Vol. I. Massachusetts:
Blackwell, 2000.

## Page 243

HM 481: READING PLATO: BETWEEN PHILOSOPHY AND LITERATURE

Instructor: Bharani Kollipara

COURSE ABSTRACT

Plato is a charming writer of philosophy in the form of dialogues. He wrote more than thirty
dialogues, ranging from short to long ones, which deal with almost all the important problems
and topics of philosophy. More importantly, all his dialogues contain, in varying degrees,
literary embellishments in the form of striking character portrayals, powerful metaphors and
analogies, drama and vivid exchanges and so on. In this course, we’ll study a few Platonic
dialogues with the aim of understanding Plato’s controversial views on politics and democracy.

We will study the following dialogues:

1. Apology
2. Gorgias
3. Laches
4. The Republic

Secondary Readings:
Monoson, Sarah. Plato’s Democratic Entanglements.
Allen, Danielle, S.,Why Plato Wrote, Malden, MA: Wiley-Blackwell.,2010
Kraut, Richard. Cambridge Companion to Plato. Cambridge: Cambridge University press.
Wolin, Sheldon. Politics and Vision
Euben, Peter. The Tragedy of Political Theory

Mode of Teaching: The course will be organized around several issues which arise from each
dialogue we study. The lectures will help you in critically approaching the assigned readings.
Discussions are aimed at targeting the specific aspects of the readings, such as ideas, difficult
passages and arguments. Freewheeling discussions are not encouraged at all. There will be
periodic quizzes. Independent study and initiative will be highly encouraged and the required
guidance would be provided.

Writing: You will be asked to write three short papers, varying between1000 to 3000 words
each, over the course of the semester. The word/page limit will be specified at the time of
assigning the task.

EVALUATION
The first paper will count for 30%, second paper for 40% and the final paper for 30%.

PLAGIARISM AND ACADEMIC INTEGRITY
Submitting someone else’s work as your own, or using others’ words and phrases without
proper citation, is a violation of norms of academic integrity, and may result in penalties up to
and including a failing grade in the course. Genuine effort and commitment will have direct
bearing on the evaluation.

## Page 244

HM414 (Open Elective, Winter 2025-26)
A Beginner’s Introduction to the Psyche
Instructor: Dr. Jenson Joseph
Course format: 3 hours of lectures (3-0-0-3)
Pre-requisites: HSS core courses
Course outline:
There are two primary approaches to the entity at the centre of this course — the psyche.
One understands it as the site that constitutes human subjectivity, imbuing our
experience of life and the world with meaning and value. The other views it as the site of
pathological issues. These approaches have generated two distinct intellectual discourses
on the workings of the psyche: psychoanalysis and psychology. While the latter is largely
a clinical practice focused on helping people cope with the pathologies of the mind,
psychoanalysis has evolved as a powerful explanatory and interpretative framework,
offering a theory of the psyche as a generative domain through which virtually all aspects
of life — and the social, cultural, and political formations arising from it — can be
understood. This course introduces foundational concepts from these disciplines
through a discussion of the seminal theories proposed by Sigmund Freud (1856–1939)
and Jacques Lacan (1901–1981).
We will explore Freud’s theories of the unconscious, the pleasure principle, the Oedipus
complex, and the triadic structure of the id, ego, and superego. We will also examine
Lacan’s concepts of the Mirror Stage, alienation, and his schema of the Symbolic,
Imaginary, and the Real. Cultural and media texts, literary works, and discussions of
current events will be used to introduce these complex concepts and help students
develop a basic understanding of the human psyche, its operations, and why it matters to
study them. The course will also delve into methods of applying the psychoanalytic
framework to evolve understandings and interpretations of socio-political and cultural
phenomena, especially when they involve matters of the spirit. These discussions will
build toward a thematic focus on socio-cultural transformations in the age of algorithms
and artificial intelligence, examined from the vantage point of their impact on the psyche
and on the constitution of the modern subject, in contrast to the era of industrial
capitalism in which classical psychoanalytic theories were devised.
Course Outcome: At the basic level, this course is meant to encourage students to
develop an interest in key concepts in the discourses on the human psyche by exploring
immediately relatable questions such as: Why do human beings experience adult life as a
landscape of complex emotions and inner conflicts that defy rational explanation? Why

## Page 245

do we struggle to control, express, or even recognize our desires? What is the origin of
our dreams? What makes us dependent on others or objects, sometimes to our own
detriment? Why is “madness” a uniquely human condition—seemingly tied to our
capacity for reason? Why do we often feel we lack full control over our will and
consciousness, as though an alien force resides within us?
By helping students develop perspectives that enable them to engage such questions, the
course equips them to understand the human psyche as a powerful faculty whose
operations produce both meaning and value as well as potential pathologies. In an age
when self-care and self-management are fetishized as pathways to well-being, this course
encourages students to reflect on the self, society, and the structures that shape psychic
formations. For participants who show particular interest, the course will also introduce
procedures for interpreting socio-cultural phenomena from a psychoanalytic perspective.
Course material:
Berg, H. de. (2004). Freud’s theory and its uses in literary and cultural studies: An introduction.
Camden House.
Lear, J. (2008). Freud. Routledge.
Phillips, A. (Ed.). (2006). The Sigmund Freud reader. Penguin Books.
Roudinesco, É. (2014). Lacan: In spite of everything. Verso Books.
Žižek, S. (2001). Enjoy your symptom! Jacques Lacan in Hollywood and out. Routledge.

## Page 247

Course name: Technology and the Making of Modern India
Course code: HM 495
Course type: Open elective
Instructor: Purbasha Das
Credit: 3-0-0-3
Semester: Winter Semester 2026

Course Outline
This course seeks to explore the various connotations of technology and comprehend how it
came to be seen as a defining feature of modernity. Technologies and scientific thinking were
indispensable for the building of the modern Indian nation state. The objective is to observe
how the nationalist and the colonial understanding of science and technology relate to each
other, as well as how the governing classes’ perspective changes over time.  The Global South
and the Global North now engage in an interchange of labour and knowledge instead of the
former metropole and colony. The readings will allow the students to reflect on the
Eurocentric notion that scientific and technological knowledge always flows from the Global
North to the Global South. The goal is to understand the uniqueness of the Indian experience
of science and technology while at the time understanding whether this experience can be
called a part of the broader global realm of science and technology. The readings explore the
interconnection between science and technology and larger processes of nationalism,
colonialism and industrialization. They also point to other intersections with environment,
labour and health. The key takeaway of the course is that technology is imbued with meaning
and technological change impacts and is shaped by all levels of society.

Aims and Objectives

 The course will enable students to think critically about technology beyond its
technical applications.
 The students will be introduced to a historical understanding of science and
technology in the post-colonial period.
 Class discussions will unpack key concepts like nation, modernity, labour and
technological progress.
 The students will be able to understand the favourable and unfavourable impact of
technology on the economy, environment and other facets of society. The course will
aid them in thinking about developing technology that is beneficial for different
sections of society.
 The writing assignments and project will improve their written and oral
communication skills.

HM 477 The English Novel: Form and History
Course Placement: The English Novel is an Open Elective (HSS) for VI & VIII Semester BTech
Students
Course Format: 3 lectures per week
Course Content:
This is a reading-intensive course. We will read 3–4 novels from the 18th, 19th and the 20th
centuries, classics of the English speaking world in the context of the social, political and economic
conditions in which they were written. We will consider the impact of these conditions especially on
the formal elements of the novel in the different phases of its development.
The readings for the course will tentatively be selected from the following set of texts: Daniel
Defoe’s Robinson Crusoe, Jane Austen’s Emma / Pride and Prejudice / Mansfield Park, Charles
Dickens’ Great Expectations / David Copperfield, Joseph Conrad’s Heart of Darkness, E. M.
Forster’s A Passage to India and Salman Rushdie’s Midnight’s Children.
Course Outcomes:
●​ Recognise the connection between literary form and the context in which texts are
produced.
●​ Develop the ability to recognise and analyse the narrative techniques employed in texts.
●​ Develop an understanding of concepts like imperialism, colonialism, enlightenment, realism,
modernism, postmodernism, postcolonialism
Assessment Method: 1 assignment and 2 exams and marks for class participation (the mode of
assessment is subject to change)
Attendance Requirement: 70% attendance compulsory

## Page 255

HM412 World Literature in Short Fiction
(Winter 2026)

Instructor: Satvik Gupta, 3204, Faculty Block-3
Course Placement: HASS Elective/Open Elective, B.Tech. (Semester - VI)
Course format: 3 Lecture hours
Course content: “The experience of reading world literature is a form of travel—a mode of
cultural encounter that presents us with languages, cultural norms, customs and ideas that may
be unfamiliar to us, even strange. As readers, each time we begin to read a new work, we put
ourselves in the role of a traveller in a foreign land, trying to understand its practices and
values and hoping to feel, to some degree and in some way, connected to and welcome among
the people we meet there” (The Norton Anthology of World Literature).
Following an introductory discourse on the history and overarching tradition of world
literature, this course will limit its scope to the texts produced during the nineteenth and
twentieth centuries. This period of world history bore witness to radical change and profound
upheaval, marked by world wars, the fall of empires, the rise of new ideologies, and
unprecedented technological advancements. This course, therefore, explores how authors
across the globe utilised the short story form to capture the anxieties and transformations of the
world around them. The readings are organised into four thematic modules that position the
‘modern individual’ against the bureaucratic city, the legacy of the empire, linguistic labyrinths
and the frailty of borders. Through this eclectic mix of narratives, learners are expected to
cultivate a deeper appreciation for the short story form as a global medium of artistic
expression.
Note: This course demands rigorous reading. Students will be required to read all the primary
texts mentioned below. Furthermore, the attendance benchmark for this course is 60%;
attendance below this threshold will result in a reduction of the grade.
Course Objectives:
This course aims to:
●​ Situate key works of short fiction within their broader historical and cultural contexts.
●​ Explore the development of major literary movements through representative texts.
●​ Cultivate skills in close reading and critical analysis.
●​ Present a diverse selection of short fiction from various global traditions to highlight the
richness of modern world literature.

1.​ Course Title
Indian Diaspora and Transnationalism: An Introduction
2.​ Credit Structure
Lecture hours per week: 3
Tutorial hours per week: 0

Total Credits: 3
3.​ Course Code

4.​ Program/Semester
Winter Semester 2026

courses
None
7.​ Foundation for

8.​ Abstract Content
According to the Ministry of External Affairs of India, thirty-two million two
hundred eighty-five thousand four hundred twenty-five Indians live abroad,
making it the largest diaspora in the world. Indians have existed transnationally
for centuries. Although initially, it was mostly through trade, the concept of
international migration is familiar to India. The nature of migration changed
over time and circumstances. Various cultural, political, and economic
developments transformed the social rubric of India. As a result, the process of
migration emerged as one of the means to tackle those unexpected challenges.
India’s first major tryst with migration began during the 19th-century colonial
period, when people from different states migrated to several other ‘colonies’ of
the British as indentured labourers. Over the years, the nature of diasporic
communities became more layered, as the individuals of Indian origin made
formidable ‘connections’ with other members of the community– both in their
host land and homeland. Indians across the diaspora are linked by a shared
‘Indian-ness’, an enduring sense of cultural belonging, and the willingness to

## Page 260

maintain a close association with home. This, however, does not come without
its own set of drawbacks - for members of diaspora, homeland, and ‘hostland’.
The course is designed to familiarise students and critically engage with the
topics of present-day diaspora, transnationalism, and international migration.
Comprehensive themes include the formation and historical background of the
Indian diaspora, overseas experiences of Indians, links with homeland,  kinship,
and ethnicity.
Suggested Text book(s) Select books for reference only; a separate course-specific reading list is
attached:
Brah, Avtar. 2005. Cartographies of Diaspora: Contesting Identities.
N.p.: Taylor & Francis.
Bauböck, Rainer, and Thomas Faist, eds. 2010. Diaspora and
Transnationalism: Concepts, Theories and Methods. N.p.: Amsterdam
University Press.
Cohen, Robin. 2002. Global Diasporas: An Introduction. N.p.: Taylor
& Francis.
Lal, Brij V., Peter Reeves, and Rajesh Rai, eds. 2006. The Encyclopedia
of the Indian Diaspora. N.p.: University of Hawaii Press.
​Shukla, Sandhya R. 2003. India Abroad: Diasporic Cultures of Postwar
America and England. N.p.: Princeton University Press.
Vertovec, Steven, and Robin Cohen, eds. 1999. Migration, Diasporas,
and Transnationalism. N.p.: Edward Elgar

The Indian diaspora:
How did it come to
be?

The introductory section explains the notion of  ‘diaspora’
and ‘diasporic communities’. Ethnographic case studies
central to the emergence and development of ‘Diaspora’ are
discussed. Following this, the origins and the formation of
the Indian diaspora will be traced across various phases -
ancient, modern, and post-modern. Readings will be divided
into the following categories:
1)​ What and who does the term ‘Diaspora’ mean?
2)​ Emergence of the Indian Diaspora
10

To
Be
Here and
There,
At
Once:
Introduction
to
Transnationalism
As the diasporic communities formed and sustained
themselves abroad, the consistent pattern of maintaining a
‘connection’ with the place of origin,  in any form - physical
or virtual, became a striking feature. This process is known
as ‘transnationalism’. This section introduces and explores
the various nuances of this phenomenon, such as are all
diasporic communities are  transnational. Why do diasporic
communities want to be transnational? Are all generations of
diaspora transnational? Readings will be grouped under the
following categories:
1)​ What is transnationalism? What does it mean to have
a ‘simultaneous existence’?
2)​ Indian Diaspora and the effect of transnationalism on
everyday life
10

Longing to Belong?:
Unpacking
the
Diasporic Identity

The process of migration affixes the importance of an
identity that is based on ethnicity, the label obtained from
“who you are” and “where you are from”. Though the
answers to those questions may not be essentially related to
the ethnic origin of a person, the subjectivity that results from
such kind of labelling leads to conflict. This conflict
manifests itself in different ways – transnational activities,
love for the homeland, and a sense of belonging to it.
1)​ Multiculturalism
2)​ Class and Caste in Diaspora - Select Case Studies

This section explains the two-way exchange between the
Bollywood Cinema and the Diaspora. The first part explains
how the Indian diaspora is portrayed on the screen and how
diasporic communities and members relate to ‘Bollywood’.
The concepts of kinship, the ‘everyday life’, relationships,
and hybrid identities among the diaspora will be discussed
through the screening of select movies (mentioned in the
course reading list).
1)​ Introduction to Bollywood and Indian Diaspora
2)​ Movie Screening(s)
10
Outcomes and
Objectives
As an introductory course, the aim is to familiarise students and help them
critically engage with concepts of diaspora, transnationalism and international
migration.  The four themes explained as a part of this course attempt to trace
the journey of the members of the diaspora to express themselves - collectively
and individually, their position in the ‘host land’ and their changing equation
with the concept of ‘home’ and ‘homeland’. The course also discusses the
formation of migrant subjectivity and the factors that impact it, thereby
addressing the various concerns that the members of the diaspora navigate with,
in their daily lives. The objective is also to understand how the Indian
diaspora’s engagement with their place of origin changed over the years and the
complex reality of being ‘diasporic’.
Comments

Second In-Semester Examination - 35%
Group Assignment - 30%

Dhirubhai Ambani University

Elective Course: HM 413: Knowledge and Identity in Three Modern Indian Novels

An Open Elective Course for the Senior Undergraduate/Postgraduate Students in
the School of Technology
Course Format: 3 Credits. 3 Lectures per Week

Prerequisites: An interest in the question of Identity, the traditional knowledge systems in India,
along with some literary sensibility in the minds of the applicants for this course.
Course Content: This course has been visualised for undergraduate students without an
intermediate academic background in the liberal arts stream. It aims to encourage and
contextualize some of the natural questions that might arise in a young mind regarding
individual identity, as framed in Indian traditions and knowledge systems.
This course belongs to the academic category of the Indian Knowledge System (IKS).
This course will examine the concept of individual/communitarian identity within the Indian
Knowledge System, which originated in the Indian subcontinent and evolved over time
through interactions with other schools of thought, facilitated by political and cultural
exchanges spanning two thousand years. This question of identity and knowledge systems
will be explored through its depiction in three iconic modern Indian novels:
Saraswatichandra, Gora, and Aag Ka Dariya (River of Fire), written by Govardhanram Tripathi
in Gujarati, Rabindranath Tagore in Bengali, and Qurratalain Hyder in Urdu, respectively. The
course shall engage with their English translations, but students are encouraged to read
them in their original versions depending on their competency in Gujarati, Bangla, and Urdu.
It is essential to note that the course will not focus on a close reading of the entire body of
the texts to be studied, especially given the considerable length of the texts in question. The
course will focus on the theme of the course as explored in the three novels rather than on
the study of the literary aspects of the novels in their entirety.
Within this broad framework of the Indian Knowledge system, this course, visualised around
the concept of Identity as imagined and defined in Indian knowledge system, aims to
understand their relevance, as well as their outline, substance, and significance in a fast-
changing present, and in the Indian context particularly, their epistemic position in the
context of representation and participation across genders, classes and communities. The
course also incorporates concepts such as truth, conscience, violence/non-violence,
responsibility, and deprivation, from the perception of their specific natures in Indian

## Page 269

2

knowledge systems in interaction with modern thought, as depicted in these three important
works by three influential figures in Indian literary history.
Primary Readings with a thematic focus
Govardhanram Tripathi, Saraswatichandra.
Rabindranath Tagore, Gora.
Qurratulain Hyder, River of Fire.

[Primary readings will be provided electronically at the commencement, and Secondary
readings progressively during the course.]

LEARNING OUTCOMES: The course aims to acquaint students with the formative themes that these
three thinkers/authors have explored and contributed to the modern concept of India, and to
identify their central arguments about identity as defined within the Indian knowledge system.
Students are encouraged to raise their individual questions regarding the art and arguments of
these three authors.
COURSE REQUIREMENTS
Attendance and Participation: A minimum attendance rate of 60% is compulsory. The
attendance policy will be strictly enforced, and students who fall short of the required
attendance percentile will be subject to a grade reduction in accordance with the attendance
policy. The success of the course depends significantly on students' engagement with the
readings and with one another in class.
Mode of Teaching: The course will be organized around the concept of Indian identity as
explored by the three authors chosen for study, and divided into four broad modules. The first
module will consist of drawing the background of the course, identifying the main strands of
thought emerging in modern India within the broad intellectual context of the meeting of
cultures that took place in the Indian subcontinent in the preceding centuries, and defining the
characteristics of Indian traditions and their knowledge system. The remaining three modules
will be thematically centred around the three novels, respectively. The lectures will involve
engagement with the themes, along with readings specifically focused on the central theme of
the course.
EVALUATION: The first evaluation, in the form of multiple-choice questions covering the first
three modules, and accounting for 30% of the total marks, will be conducted as part of the
Second In-Semester examinations. Group presentations, to be made by students, will be
scheduled during the last phase of lectures and will account for an additional 10% of the marks.
Students are encouraged to make presentations based on their personal interpretations of the
theme and content of the novels or create scenarios that relate to the themes of the three
novels. The End-of-Semester examination, comprising multiple-choice, short-answer, and one
detailed-answer question, will cover the entire course and account for 60% of the marks.

## Page 270

3

PLAGIARISM AND ACADEMIC INTEGRITY: Using words and phrases of authors without proper citation is
considered a violation of norms of academic integrity and is strictly discouraged. Genuine effort
and commitment will be duly appreciated.
COURSE OUTCOMES: The course aims to achieve the following outcomes:
•
Introduction to Indian Knowledge System.

•
Understanding the ethical human responsibility for nurturing a peaceful comity of
races and communities.
•
The novels to be studied reflect an understanding of knowledge as a repository,
imagination, introspection, and evolution, and identity as a combination of a
historical core and reflective process evolving through advancing knowledge arising
from interaction among human communities. This concept of knowledge and identity
forms an intrinsic part of Indian knowledge systems. Scholars and thinkers in India
generally envisioned a universe intricately connected in both its creation and
destruction. Therefore, all human activity was, in some way or other, part of this
quest to discover the self and the world. Each individual developed with the support
of others, whether it was visible or not.
•
Knowing and understanding this fact was one of the highest functions of human
consciousness. One should celebrate one’s heritage, but should remain mindful of its
universal human characteristics. Awareness of this fact is best reflected in the
transcendence of chauvinism and intolerance, as well as in sensitivity to global ecological
concerns, the commonality of concerns, and transnational moral claims to basic amenities
across cultures. The course will highlight the importance of this formative and
pervasive principle of life.

***

## Page 271

HM 402. Publics in South Asia: Contemporary Perspectives
Winter 2025-26
Instructor: Dr. Shefali Jha

Course Placement: HM 402 is an Open Elective
Course Format: 3 hours of lectures each week.
Pre-requisites: None

Course Content
We all assume that we are part of something called ‘the public’, and assume its existence in
our roles as students, teachers, ‘fans’, citizens/ ‘netizens’, readers, spectators and so on.
Nobody invites us to become members, nor can anyone take away our participation in the
public, active or passive. Publics are at the heart of the democratic imagination: Just as we
cannot imagine modern politics without democracy, democracy has little meaning without ‘the
public.’
Why and how did this come to be? What the various practices and technologies through which
we come to be members of publics? What are the spaces and representations of people, cultures
and events, through which we may identify and analyse publics? Who draws the line between
the public and the private, and why is that line important? What significance do national
boundaries and histories have for such analyses? This course is based on exploring these
fundamental questions with the help of readings taken from recent ethnographic work by
anthropologists of Bangladesh, Nepal, Pakistan, India and Sri Lanka, aside from historical
accounts and political theory. We will see whether there are threads of similarity which run
through the social worlds of class, caste, gender, and sexuality in the region, and how these
affect our ways of thinking about publics, publicity, and privacy. We will read about and work
with the written word, films, music and radio performances to discuss these questions.

Course Material (Selected):
1. Global Digital Futures. Edited by Aswin Punthambekar and Sriram Mohan. University
of Michigan Press, 2017.
2. Habermas and the Public Sphere. Edited by Craig Calhoun. MIT Press, 1992.
3. Music and Dance as Everyday South Asia. Edited by Sarah H. Morelli and Zoe
Sherinian. OUP, 2024.

## Page 272

4. Kant, Immanuel. 1784. "An Answer to the Question: What is Enlightenment?"
Translated by James Schmidt. In What is Enlightenment? Eighteenth Century Answers
and Twentieth-Century Questions, edited by James Schmidt. University of California
Press, 1996.
5. Kaviraj, Sudipta. 2014. ‘A Strange Love of the Land: Identity, Poetry and Politics in
the (Un)Making of South Asia.’ South Asia Multidisciplinary Academic Journal
[Online], 10. http://journals.openedition.org/samaj/3826; doi : 10.4000/samaj.3756
6. Warner, Michael. “Publics and Counterpublics.” Public Culture 14 (1): 49- 90.

Method of Assessment: Class Participation (20%); Class Presentation + write-up (10 + 20%);
Reviews + Response papers (20%); final paper to be written in class (30%).
Participation will be judged by attendance, tasks, as well as helpful and relevant interventions
in class discussion.
Plagiarism on assignments and unacknowledged, thoughtless use of AI tools will result in a
failing grade for the course.

Course Outcomes: On successfully completing the course, students will have a basic
familiarity with India’s neighbouring states and the socio-cultural fabric of South Asian
societies. They will be able to reflect on their own experience, perspectives and responsibilities
as future knowledge workers and citizens. They will also be able to read and think with research
material in the social sciences, becoming conversant with ethnography as a method of research.
Their writing and analytical skills should improve, and they should learn the skill of
communicating complex ideas to different kinds of audiences.

1. Course Title: Computational Finance

6. Instructor’s Name with email:
Jaideep Mulherkar
Jaideep_mulherkar@daiict.ac.in

7. Course Objectives:
A derivative is a financial instrument that derives its price from an underlying asset like
a stock. An example is a forward contract where two parties agree to buy/sell an asset
at a fixed price at some future date. Another example is that of a call option where one
party (option buyer) agrees to buy an asset from another party (option seller) at a future
date. But in this case the contract is asymmetric, that is, the option buyer has the right
but no obligation to exercise the option whereas the option seller is obligated to exercise
the option at end date. How do you value such a financial contract? The value of the
option is the option price of the call option. There is a nice mathematical theory of option
pricing which is based on principles of efficient markets and mathematical theory of
probability. The Black-Scholes-Merton model of pricing a European call option is a
landmark work in financial and economic theory which received the Noble price for
economics in 1997. Current financial markets are replete with complicated financial
instruments; exotic options and option exchanges are common place. Everyday billions
of dollars of options are traded on various exchanges of the world. Options are traded
everyday on various underlying assets like stock, currency or commodities. This course
introduces the student to the theory of option pricing and financial derivatives.

b. Options, Futures and Other Derivtives J.C. Hull and S. Basu
c. Stochastic calculus for finance I Shreve

Introduction to Robotics

Course Code
IE410
Credit Structure
3-0-2-4 (L-T-P-C)
Category
Core / Elective
Semester
Winter Semester (AY 24-25)
Program
Introduction to Robotics is an ICT and Technical Elective course for students
of BTech ICT, RAS (Robotics and Automation Systems) minor and Honours
programs. It is also offered as an elective for MTech program.
Prerequisites
-
Course Objectives/
Brief Course
Description
This course provides a comprehensive introduction to the exciting and
interdisciplinary field of robotics, combining theory and hands-on practice to
enable students to build and program robotic systems from scratch. Students
will gain foundational knowledge in key topics such as coordinate
transformations, kinematics, dynamics, control, and robotics graphics. The
course covers advanced concepts like trajectory planning, sensor integration,
and hybrid control strategies, preparing students for real-world applications.
Through interactive lectures and laboratory sessions, students will design,
implement, and analyze robotic systems, applying modern tools and
programming techniques using software packages like MATLAB-Simulink
and Python. By the end of the course, students will acquire practical skills
and theoretical insights required for careers in robotics, automation, and
related industries, while also addressing societal and ethical considerations
in engineering solutions. This course is ideal for students seeking to explore
robotics as a technical elective, with applications in academia and industry.
Course Objectives
1. To provide students with foundational knowledge of robotics, including
coordinate transformations, kinematics, dynamics, and control.
2. To enable students to design, build, and program robotic systems from
scratch, emphasizing practical applications and theoretical principles.
3. To introduce students to sensors, actuators, and power management
circuits essential for robotic systems.
4. To foster skills in robotic computing and simulation using tools like
MATLAB-Simulink and Python for real-world robotic applications.
5. To prepare students for interdisciplinary challenges in robotics and
automation, aligning with industry needs and societal development.
Evaluation/ Grading
Policy
Evaluation scheme:
(i) First and Second In-Sem Exam: 15%+15%=30%,
(ii) End-Sem Exam: 25%,
(iii) Quizzes, Assignments, (Lab/Group) Projects, 15%+05%+15%=35%,

## Page 279

(iv) Labs: 10%, and
(v) Attendance 0% (Zero %).
Course Materials/
References
1. Introduction to Robotics: Analysis, Control, Applications, 3rd Ed.
Saeed B. Niku, Wiley, 2019.
2. Robotics - Modelling, Planning and Control, 1st Ed. B. Siciliano, L.
Sciavicco, L. Villani, and G. Oriolo, Springer, 2009.
3. Introduction to Robotics: Mechanics and Control, 3rd Ed. John J.
Craig, Addison-Wesley Publishing Company, 2003.
4. Robotics, Vision and Control, Fundamental Algorithms in Python,
3rd edition, Peter Corke, Springer, 2023.
5. Introduction to Autonomous Mobile Robots, 2nd Edition, Roland
Siegwart, Illah R. Nourbakhsh, and Davide Scaramuzza, The MIT Press,
2011.
6. Robot Manipulator Control: Theory and Practice, Frank L. Lewis,
Darren M. Dawson, and Chaouki T. Abdallah, CRC Press, 2003.
7. Modern Robotics, K.M. Lynch, Cambridge University Press, 2017.
8. Other resources like, notes, papers shared on Google Drive from time to
time.

Detailed Course
Content
APPENDIX

Course Outcomes
Upon successful completion of this course, students will be able to:
1.  Demonstrate a clear understanding of the principles of robotic architecture, kinematics,
dynamics, and robot control.
2.  Apply knowledge of coordinate transformations and Denavit-Hartenberg (D-H) parameters to
solve robotic problems involving forward and inverse kinematics.
3.  Design and implement control strategies, including PID and hybrid control, for robotic
applications.
4.  Develop and integrate sensors, actuators, and power management systems to create functional
robotic systems.
5.  Exhibit proficiency in programming robotic controllers and simulations using MATLAB-
Simulink, Python and other tools/packages.
6. Collaborate effectively in teams to design, build, and test robotic systems, fostering teamwork
and leadership skills.
7.  Analyze and solve interdisciplinary problems in robotics, demonstrating an understanding of
societal and ethical implications.
8. Use modern tools and techniques to design and simulate robotic solutions for industrial and
societal applications.
